\section{Localization and Angular Bandlimits}
\label{sec:ch44}
\ref{ch:04} asks which patterns finite hardware can generate; Section~\ref{sec:ch44} records how spatial localization and Fourier--angular duality compress high-order content before harmonic labels are trusted \cite{jackson1999}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch44
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch44-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Compact radiator in a standardized body phantom for over-the-air test]
A second bench observation matters on Pyramidal horn on a compact range. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch44} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Compact radiator in a standardized body phantom for over-the-air test]
Consider a compact radiator mounted in a standardized body phantom for over-the-air range test. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch44} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Excitation-to-flux sequences in Section~\ref{sec:ch43} name rank through singular values on bounded sources; Section~\ref{sec:ch44} translates that rank into angular language the range engineer already recognizes \cite{jackson1999}. A finite current distribution cannot localize arbitrarily in both spatial extent and angular spectrum: bandlimits are source properties imposed by compact support before any mode chart is chosen \cite{landau1962}. The laboratory sees the translation as sidelobe floors and beamwidth limits on a dish or slot, not as abstract operator compactness \cite{stratton1941}.
The subsections below translate operator rank into coordinates the range engineer already uses: beamwidth on a dish, sidelobe floors on a slot, and the transverse wavenumber content visible on a planar scan \cite{jackson1999}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch441} states spatial localization and Fourier--angular duality; Subsection~\ref{sec:ch442} separates propagating and evanescent lateral sheets; Subsection~\ref{sec:ch443} records high-$\ell$ decay on compact support; Subsection~\ref{sec:ch444} collects the finite-support geometric limitation $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}$ that bounds $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})$ \cite{stratton1941}.
\subsection{Spatial localization and Fourier-angular duality}
\label{sec:ch441}
Think of localization and directivity as two sides of one hardware coin. A compact planar radiator, a horn mouth, and a reflector rim all radiate through the same retarded export map $\mathcal{R}_\omega$, yet each presents a different spatial second moment $\mathcal{L}_{2}$ and therefore a different lateral spectrum on a scan plane \cite{jackson1999}. Narrow beams and high transverse wavenumber both demand large effective aperture: phase-only steering on fixed metal explores directions geometry already permits; it does not purchase new propagating channels beyond the support-imposed bandlimit \cite{devaney2004}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch441
\begin{physicalvignette}[Offset-fed reflector with shaped beam]
An offset-fed reflector for satellite downlink makes the localization trade tangible. The electrical outline that sets rank is the projected aperture on the sky, not the decorative radome diameter printed on a brochure \cite{harrington2001}. When designers ask for a sharper main beam without enlarging that projected area, the range response is unchanged sidelobe shoulders, not a magic narrowing \cite{jackson1999}.
Evanescent energy stored near the subreflector struts can dominate probe readings on a close scan surface while contributing negligibly to the flux integral on a far sphere \cite{stratton1941}. Reporting those near scans as ``extra modes'' without a propagating filter is the reactive mimicry failure that later sections treat as a rank-gap symptom \cite{hansen1988}.
Landau and Slepian long ago warned that finite domains cannot simultaneously localize and carry unbounded spectral spread \cite{landau1962}. Maxwell still radiates; geometry still caps what the dish can export at declared tolerance \cite{maxwell1873}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch441} states the localization and directivity constraints this reflector scene illustrates \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Physically, the question is not whether a harmonic listing can \emph{display} fine angular structure, but whether impressed currents on bounded $\Omega_s$ can \emph{populate} that structure in the propagating sector at fixed $\omega$ \cite{stratton1941}. The synthesis operator $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$---the export map $\mathcal{R}_\omega$ followed by flux-normalized chart $\mathcal{C}$---maps $\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}$ into coefficient space $\mathbb{C}^{I}$; compact support compresses its singular values before any index in $I$ acquires laboratory meaning \cite{harrington2001}. Coefficient vectors $\mathbf{c}$ are read only after that compression; lateral spectra $\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{rad}}(k_x,k_y)$ are the plane-wave face of the same constraint \cite{hansen1988}.
A source confined to \(\Omega_{s}\) cannot excite arbitrary lateral spectra on a measurement plane: spatial
confinement induces a \emph{spatial--spectral duality} that bandlimits realizable \(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(k_{x},k_{y})\)
before angular listing \(\mathbf{c}\) is formed \cite{stratton1941}. Subsection
~\ref{sec:ch441} makes that duality a realizability law on \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\).
Fix compact \(\Omega_{s}\), observation plane \(z=z_{0}\) outside \(\Omega_{s}\), and outgoing
\(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{stratton1941}. Lateral Fourier conventions follow
Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch2.plane-wave-angular-spectrum-def}--\eqref{eq:ch2.plane-wave-z-propagation} from
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch291} \cite{stratton1941}. Flux-normalized plane-wave coefficients follow
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.fourier-radiation-filter-chain} from Subsection~\ref{sec:ch381} \cite{hansen1988}.
Representation \(\mathcal{C}\) may be spherical or plane-wave; duality is stated in \((k_{x},k_{y})\) first
\cite{stratton1941}.
Localization functional. Define the spatial second moment of \(\Jimp\) about centroid \(\mathbf{r}_{c}\) by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.localization-moment}
\mathcal{L}_{2}[\Jimp]
=
\int_{\Omega_{s}}|\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}_{c}|^{2}\,|\Jimp(\mathbf{r})|^{2}\,d^{3}r,
\end{equation}
with \(\mathbf{r}_{c}\) the current centroid \cite{jackson1999}. For ball support \(B_{a}\),
\(\mathcal{L}_{2}\sim a^{2}\) sets the spatial extent that enters spectral tail decay in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.spectral-tail-energy} \cite{hansen1988}.
Let \(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{rad}}(k_{x},k_{y})\) be the radiative lateral spectrum obtained from
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.fourier-radiation-filter-chain} \cite{harrington2001}. Compact support implies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.spatial-spectral-duality}
|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{rad}}(k_{x},k_{y})|
\le
\mathcal{M}(k_{0}a)\,\Phi(k_{t}/k_{0}),
\qquad
\Phi(u)\to0\ \text{as}\ u\to\infty,
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{M}\) depending on peak \(\|\Jimp\|\) and \(\Phi\) fixed by outgoing Helmholtz symbol
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch2.helmholtz-symbol} \cite{stratton1941}.
An offset-fed reflector concentrates power in a bounded region of the $(k_{x},k_{y})$ plane before any VSH
coefficient is read \cite{stratton1941}.
Attempting to synthesize a pattern whose lateral spectrum exceeds the support-implied bound of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.spatial-spectral-duality} is equivalent to demanding evanescent sheet energy that no finite
feed on $\Omega_{s}$ can supply in the radiative sector \cite{devaney2004}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.spatial-spectral-duality} is the $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ reading of Fourier--angular duality: there is no
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) whose plane-wave content violates
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.spatial-spectral-duality} at fixed \((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\) \cite{devaney2004}. High
\(k_{t}\) sheets are suppressed not by measurement alone but by synthesis on compact \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{def:ch4.angular-bandlimit}
For tolerance \(\eta>0\), the \emph{lateral angular bandlimit} is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.lateral-bandlimit}
k_{t,\max}(\eta)
=
\sup\left\{
k_{t}:\ 
\frac{\int_{k_{t}'<k_{t}}|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{rad}}|^{2}\,dk_{x}\,dk_{y}}
{\int|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{rad}}|^{2}\,dk_{x}\,dk_{y}}
\ge 1-\eta
\right\}.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.lateral-bandlimit} names the smallest transverse wavenumber sphere containing
\((1-\eta)\) of radiative spectral weight \cite{hansen1988}. It is the continuous analogue of
\(\ell_{\max}\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.spectral-compression-def}: spherical and plane-wave bandlimits are
related by Section~\ref{sec:ch38} transformation laws, not by naive index equality \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.duality-bandlimit}
For compact \(\Omega_{s}\) with diameter \(D_{s}\) and electrical size \(\Xi\) from
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.electrical-size},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.duality-bandlimit-scaling}
k_{t,\max}(\eta)
\le
\kappa(\eta)\,k_{0}\sqrt{1+(2\pi\Xi)^{2}},
\end{equation}
with \(\kappa(\eta)\) depending only on tail budget \(\eta\) and outgoing symbol
\cite{stratton1941}. Consequently \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}]\) has
negligible content on plane-wave sheets with \(k_{t}\gg k_{t,\max}(\eta)\) at the same \(\eta\) used in
(\ref{def:ch4.radiative-rank}) \cite{devaney2004}.
Spectral decay for compact sources follows from analytic continuation of the dyadic kernel in transverse
wavenumber \cite{stratton1941}. Normalization through \(\mathcal{N}\) and boundedness of
\(\mathcal{C}\) transfer the lateral bound to coefficient norms \cite{hansen1988}. Alignment
with \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}\) follows because both count negligible directions in the same compact operator
\cite{devaney2004}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.duality-bandlimit}) is the inevitability statement of Subsection~\ref{sec:ch441}: one cannot
synthesize arbitrarily fine transverse structure from a bounded \(\Omega_{s}\) at fixed \(k_{0}\)
\cite{jackson1999}. Phase-only metasurfaces that imprint high \(k_{t}\) on \(\Sigma_{a}\) without
enlarging effective \(\mathcal{L}_{2}\) violate Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.duality-bandlimit-scaling} \cite{harrington2001}.
Duality ties electric and magnetic multipole content the way $\E$ and $\H$ swap under Maxwell duality in free space \cite{stratton1941}. Bandlimits on one sector constrain the other: a loop-dominated source cannot simultaneously mimic an electric multipole ladder beyond the same electrical size \cite{harrington2001}.

 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of l2] (kt) {$k_{t,\max}(\eta)$};

}

the spatial extent \(\mathcal{L}_{2}\) of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.localization-moment} and lateral spectral spread
\(\Delta k_{t}\) satisfy a concentration inequality of the form
Chapter~4 develops what finite geometry permits before harmonic optimization begins. The next display should be read against that question, not as an isolated definition. \cite{stratton1941}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.localization-spectrum-product}
\mathcal{L}_{2}\,\Delta k_{t}
\ge
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{loc}}(k_{0},\eta),
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{loc}}>0\) depending on tail budget \(\eta\) and outgoing symbol
\cite{stratton1941}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.localization-spectrum-product} is the synthesizable
reading of Fourier--angular duality: shrinking \(\mathcal{L}_{2}\) without widening \(\Delta k_{t}\) forces
\(\mathbf{c}\) outside \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) \cite{devaney2004}. The bound is not a measurement
uncertainty relation; it is a synthesis obstruction imposed by compact \(\operatorname{supp}\Jimp\)
\cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.super-oscillation}
If a target \(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\) requires transverse structure with effective
\(k_{t,\mathrm{req}}\gg k_{t,\max}(\eta)\) from (\ref{thm:ch4.duality-bandlimit}), then
\(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\notin\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) on the declared \((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\)
\cite{jackson1999}.
Let \(\Phi[\mathfrak{F}](k_{x},k_{y})\) denote the lateral spectrum of \(\mathfrak{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) on a
plane \(z=z_{0}\) \cite{stratton1941}. Compact \(\Omega_{s}\) imposes
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.plane-wave-support-restriction}
\operatorname{supp}\Phi\big[\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp]\big]
\subseteq
\big\{(k_{x},k_{y}):k_{t}\le k_{t,\max}(\eta)\big\},
\end{equation}
with \(k_{t,\max}\) from (\ref{thm:ch4.duality-bandlimit}) \cite{hansen1988}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.plane-wave-support-restriction} is the lateral restatement of
\(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\): synthesis targets must concentrate in the same spectral support as
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}]\) \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.bandlimit-listing-continuity}
If Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.plane-wave-support-restriction} holds and \(\mathcal{C}\) is a bounded transformation from
\(\Phi\) to \(\mathbf{c}\) as in Section~\ref{sec:ch38}, then every realizable \(\mathbf{c}\) inherits the same
\(k_{t,\max}(\eta)\) bound up to constants from basis change \cite{hansen1988}.
Boundedness of the plane-wave--VSH transform on compact domains
\cite{hansen1988}; (\ref{def:ch4.angular-bandlimit}) \cite{devaney2004}.
Regarding beamwidth--bandlimit relation, for a far-zone beam with half-power width \(\theta_{\mathrm{HP}}\), range membership on \(B_{a}\) requires
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.beamwidth-bandlimit}
\theta_{\mathrm{HP}}
\gtrsim
\frac{\lambda}{\pi D_{s}}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k_{t,\mathrm{req}}\lesssim k_{t,\max}(\eta),
\end{equation}
linking angular beam metrics to (\ref{thm:ch4.duality-bandlimit}) \cite{jackson1999}.
Super-narrow beams on electrically small apertures violate Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.beamwidth-bandlimit} before
\(\ell_{\max}\) is consulted \cite{devaney2004}.
Complete harmonic charts inherit bandlimits from support diameter: listing $\ell\le 15$ does not create $\ell=15$ export on a electrically small outline \cite{hansen1988}. The theorem prevents importing closed-cavity mode counts into open-region marketing claims \cite{jackson1999}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.min-aperture-beamwidth}
A target half-power width \(\theta_{\mathrm{target}}\) at wavelength \(\lambda\) requires
\(D_{s}\gtrsim\lambda/(\pi\theta_{\mathrm{target}})\) for \(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\)
up to constants from \(\eta\) \cite{jackson1999}.
\subsection{Evanescent and propagating components}
\label{sec:ch442}
% CH04-NARR-EXP:04.442
Near-field data taken at $kr<1$ are rich in evanescent structure that stores reactive energy locally; watt-bearing far-zone multipoles live in the propagating sheet filtered by Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-filter} \cite{stratton1941}. Super-directive pattern targets that lean on evanescent amplification fail once tail watts are integrated on $\Gamma_R$ \cite{hansen1988}.
Every compact-range campaign eventually confronts the same partition. Near-field probes at $kr<1$ resolve reactive sheets rich in evanescent lateral content $k_t>k_0$; far-zone meters on $\Gamma_R$ integrate only the propagating sector $\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{prop}}$ that carries retarded power outward \cite{stratton1941}. The laboratory mistake is to treat a dense near-field map as evidence of far-zone diversity: evanescent storage can dominate probe readings while $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]$---and hence $\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})$---depends only on propagating export after $\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}$ \cite{harrington2001}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch442
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch442-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Spherical near-field scanner with dual-polarization probes]
A second bench observation matters on Beth torsion balance in a microwave anechoic chamber. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch442} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Spherical near-field scanner with dual-polarization probes]
Consider Spherical near-field scanner with dual-polarization probes. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Evanescent fields store reactive energy near sources; propagating fields carry far-zone power . Near-field richness misleads if reported as far-zone diversity .
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch442} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Super-directive pattern targets must not be interpreted as rank enlargement. Templates that concentrate energy in $\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}$ may leave $\mathcal{A}_\infty$ nearly unchanged while reactive fraction $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ev}}/\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}$ grows; passivity and tail budgets on $\Gamma_R$ expose the gap once watts, not volts, are measured \cite{stratton1941}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch442} makes that partition quantitative: propagating realizability, evanescent invisibility on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$, and the spectral diagnostics that separate synthesis targets from near-field recovery objects \cite{devaney2004}.
Propagating $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ sector. Define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.propagating-sector}
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{prop}}
=
\{(k_{x},k_{y}):k_{t}\le k_{0},\ k_{z}\ \text{outgoing}\},
\end{equation}
using the propagating sheet of Subsection~\ref{sec:ch224} \cite{stratton1941}. Realizable radiative spectra
satisfy
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.propagating-realizable}
\operatorname{supp}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{rad}}
\subseteq
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{prop}}
\quad\text{up to null measure},
\end{equation}
because \(\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}\) annihilates evanescent content before \(\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-range membership} is the spectral form of
(\ref{prin:ch4.operator-composition}): reactive near fields may carry large \(k_{t}>k_{0}\) weight in raw
\(\mathcal{S}_\omega[\Jimp]\) but not in \(\mathbf{c}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.evanescent-sector}
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}
=
\{(k_{x},k_{y}):k_{t}>k_{0}\},
\qquad
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{ev}}
=
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}}.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.evanescent-sector} isolates reactive lateral content \cite{stratton1941}. Near-field
probes sensitive to \(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) report structure that does not enter
\(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{harrington2001}. 
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.evanescent-invisibility}
For every \(\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.evanescent-gamma-null}
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]
\ \text{independent of}\ 
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{ev}}
\quad\text{on}\ \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}},
\end{equation}
equivalently, \(\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}\) annihilates \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) content before
\(\mathcal{C}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch2.rad-projection-composition} and evanescent null
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch2.rad-null-on-evanescent} \cite{stratton1941}.
(\ref{prop:ch4.evanescent-invisibility}) forbids fitting far-zone \(\mathbf{c}\) with evanescent-only
near-field templates: such templates are recovery objects for reactive storage, not synthesis targets
\cite{stratton1941}. Super-resolution claims based solely on \(k_{t}>k_{0}\) near-field energy must
separate \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) from \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{prop}}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
For a lateral spectral density \(|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(k_{x},k_{y})|^{2}\), define propagating and evanescent
energy fractions
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.prop-ev-energy-fractions}
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}
=
\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{prop}}}\!
|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{rad}}|^{2}\,d^{2}k_{t},
\qquad
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ev}}
=
\int_{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}}\!

\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{prop}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) from Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-sector} and

does not imply large synthesizable \(\mathbf{c}\) \cite{devaney2004}.

Figure~\ref{fig:ch4.prop-ev-partition} summarizes the synthesis path: $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ optimizes $\mathbf{c}$ on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$, not on the full near-field spectrum
\cite{stratton1941}. Super-directivity designs that concentrate near-field evanescent energy may leave
$\mathbf{c}$ nearly unchanged while $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ev}}$ grows \cite{devaney2004}.

Let \({\Jimp}_{A},{\Jimp}_{B}\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) satisfy

distinguish \({\Jimp}_{A}\) from \({\Jimp}_{B}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Superdirectivity and reactive storage. Superdirective templates concentrate field energy in evanescent sectors \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) while leaving
\(\mathcal{A}_{\infty}\) nearly unchanged \cite{stratton1941}. The $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ reading is
sharp: \(\|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{ev}}\|\) may grow without enlarging \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\)
(\ref{prop:ch4.evanescent-invisibility}) \cite{devaney2004}. Passivity caps total stored energy through
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.passivity-screen} even when \(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) is large \cite{stratton1941}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.superdirectivity-obstruction}
\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ev}}}{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}}
\gg1
\quad\not\Longrightarrow\quad
\dim\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\ \text{enlarged},
\end{equation}
because \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) factors through \(\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.superdirectivity-obstruction} forbids interpreting reactive enhancement as additional far-zone
synthesis rank \cite{hansen1988}.
Decompose the lateral spectrum of \(\mathcal{S}_\omega[\Jimp]\) into propagating and evanescent partitions
Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch4.prop-ev-energy-fractions}. The synthesis partition is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.synthesis-spectrum-partition}
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{syn}}
=
\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\,\widetilde{\mathbf{A}},
\qquad
\|\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{syn}}\|^{2}
=
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}^{\mathrm{(rad)}},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}^{\mathrm{(rad)}}\) the radiative propagating energy \cite{stratton1941}.
Every \(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) is determined by \(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{syn}}\) alone;
reactive sheets \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) are measurement-only \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.prop-sector-equivalence}
\(\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) satisfy \(\mathbf{c}_{1}=\mathbf{c}_{2}\) if and
only if \(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{syn}}^{(1)}=\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{syn}}^{(2)}\) on
\(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{prop}}\) up to gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Distinct near fields with
identical \(\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{syn}}\) differ only in \(\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) storage
\cite{harrington2001}.
(\ref{prop:ch4.evanescent-invisibility}) and boundedness of \(\mathcal{C}\) on
\(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Propagating sectors on the observation sphere are those that carry retarded power through the export map; evanescent sectors store reactive energy near the source \cite{stratton1941}. Near-field scans rich in evanescent content must be filtered before far-zone rank is inferred \cite{devaney2004}.
\subsection{Decay of high-order angular content}
\label{sec:ch443}
Section~\ref{sec:ch441} bandlimited lateral spectra; the spherical listing inherits the same physics through high-angular-order tail decay \cite{hansen1988}. On a ball $B_a$ of radius $a$, electrical size $k_0 a$ sets how rapidly coefficients $\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}$ must fall once $\ell$ exceeds the support scale: sectors with $\ell\gg k_0 a$ carry exponentially suppressed export, visible on compact ranges as sidelobe floors that no feed optimization on fixed outline can erase \cite{jackson1999}.

On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
High-order angular content decays on compact supports . Tail floors appear as shoulders when synthesis ignores them .

The significance of the tail law for realizability is direct. Tail budget $\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}$ names the watt fraction permitted beyond truncation $\ell_{\max}$; remainder operator $\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}$ from Chapter~3 carries the discarded power \cite{stratton1941}. A sidelobe campaign that assigns appreciable weight to $\ell\ge 8$ on a $\lambda/8$ disk is therefore not a feed-network problem alone---it conflicts with $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}$ before MoM inversion begins \cite{chu1948}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch443} states the decay envelope, links tail mass $\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{tail}}$ to synthesis error, and records the tail exponent $\nu_{\mathrm{tail}}(\mathbf{c})$ laboratories can verify on measured $\mathbf{c}$ \cite{hansen1988}.
for \(\ell>k_{0}a\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay}
|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|
\le
\mathcal{C}_{\ell}(k_{0}a)\,\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}\,
\ell^{-1}\exp(-\ell/k_{0}a),
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{C}_{\ell}\) depending on polarization sector \(\sigma\) and normalization gauge
\cite{stratton1941}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} uses the engineering tail law of
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch362} as a $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ inequality: coefficients above the envelope cannot be synthesized on
\(B_{a}\) at fixed \(k_{0}\) \cite{harrington2001}. The bound aligns with
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.compression-error-import} when \(\{\mathbf{u}_{n}\}\) are VSH modes
\cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.ell-tail-monotone}
Let \(\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\) be the truncation remainder Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.truncation-remainder-energy}. Then
\(\|\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}\) decreases monotonically in \(\ell_{\max}\), and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ellmax-tail-link}
\|\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}^{2}
\le
\sum_{\ell>\ell_{\max}}(2\ell+1)\max_{m,\sigma}|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|^{2},
\end{equation}
with equality in the aligned VSH gauge \cite{hansen1988}.
Orthogonality (\ref{thm:ch3.vsh-orthogonality}) and Parseval
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.mode-power-from-coefficient} \cite{hansen1988}.
(\ref{prop:ch4.ell-tail-monotone}) links spherical bandlimits to watt residuals: raising \(\ell_{\max}\)
reduces truncation error monotonically but cannot create content forbidden by Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay}
\cite{harrington2001}. Targets with plateau tails at \(\ell\gg k_{0}a\) lie outside
\(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) on \(B_{a}\) \cite{jackson1999}.
Summing Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} over \((m,\sigma)\) at fixed \(\ell\) gives the partial mode-block energy
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-block-energy}
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
=
\sum_{m,\sigma}|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|^{2}
\le
(2\ell+1)\,\mathcal{C}_{\ell}^{2}(k_{0}a)\,\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}^{2}\,
\ell^{-2}\exp(-2\ell/k_{0}a),
\end{equation}
with the \((2\ell+1)\) degeneracy factor of spherical harmonics \cite{hansen1988}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-block-energy} converts pointwise tail bounds into cumulative watt residuals: the tail projector
\(\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{\max}}\) of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.ellmax-truncation-projector} removes blocks \(\mathcal{E}_{\ell}\)
for \(\ell>\ell_{\max}\) with controlled error \cite{hansen1988}. Targets whose \(\mathcal{E}_{\ell}\) plateau above
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-block-energy} for \(\ell\gg k_{0}a\) are outside \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) of
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch444} \cite{devaney2004}.

Figure~\ref{fig:ch4.high-ell-tail} connects spherical listing to radiative rank fixed by geometry: when \(\{\mathbf{u}_{n}\}\) align with
VSH modes, \(\ell_{\max}\) and \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) count the same negligible directions up to gauge
\cite{hansen1988}. (\ref{prop:ch4.geometry-spectral-equivalence}) links
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-block-energy} and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.compression-error-import} \cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.tail-plateau}
If \(|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|\ge c_{0}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}\) for all \(\ell\in[\ell_{1},\ell_{2}]\) with
\(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}\gg k_{0}a\) and \(c_{0}>0\) independent of \(\ell\), then
\(\mathbf{c}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\eta,\varepsilon)\) for any \(\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}\) satisfying
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.spectral-compression-def} on \(B_{a}\) at fixed \(k_{0}\) \cite{hansen1988}.
Plateau tails violate Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} and inflate
\(\|\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}\) in (\ref{prop:ch4.ell-tail-monotone})
\cite{stratton1941}.
Cumulative tail mass. Summing Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-block-energy} over \(\ell>\ell_{\max}\) defines
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.cumulative-tail-mass}
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{tail}}(\ell_{\max})
=
\sum_{\ell>\ell_{\max}}(2\ell+1)\,
\max_{m,\sigma}|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|^{2},
\qquad
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{tail}}
\le
\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}\,\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}^{2}
\ \Longrightarrow\
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}},
\end{equation}
linking tail mass directly to Definition~\ref{def:ch4.finite-support-restriction} \cite{hansen1988}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.cumulative-tail-mass} is the watt-domain verification companion to
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay}: pointwise bounds integrate into cumulative tail budgets \cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.tail-mass-monotone}
\(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{tail}}(\ell_{\max})\) decreases monotonically in \(\ell_{\max}\), and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.tail-mass-synthesis-error}
\|\mathbf{c}-\mathbf{c}^{(\ell_{\max})}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}^{2}
=
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{tail}}(\ell_{\max})
\end{equation}
in the aligned VSH gauge \cite{hansen1988}. Targets with
\(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{tail}}(\ell_{\max})>\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}^{2}\) at every admissible
\(\ell_{\max}(k_{0}a)\) lie outside \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) on \(B_{a}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
(\ref{prop:ch4.ell-tail-monotone}) and Parseval
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.mode-power-from-coefficient} \cite{hansen1988}.
Define the tail exponent
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.tail-exponent}
\nu_{\mathrm{tail}}(\mathbf{c})
=
\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}
\frac{\log|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|}{\ell},
\end{equation}
with the limit taken over the dominant \((m,\sigma)\) sector at each \(\ell\) \cite{hansen1988}. On \(B_{a}\),
realizable \(\mathbf{c}\) satisfy \(\nu_{\mathrm{tail}}(\mathbf{c})\le-1/(k_{0}a)\) by Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay}
\cite{jackson1999}. Faster-than-envelope decay is admissible; slower decay violates
\(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) \cite{hansen1988}.
Tail mass monotone decay means discarded high-$\ell$ sectors carry measurable watt remainder---shoulders and cross-polar lobes on real dishes \cite{harrington2001}. Truncation is therefore an engineering choice with a wattmeter reading, not an abstract Hilbert-space cutoff \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.tail-exponent-obstruction}
If \(\nu_{\mathrm{tail}}(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}})>-1/(k_{0}a)\), then
\(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\notin\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) on \(B_{a}\) at fixed \(k_{0}\), independent
of phase-only retuning on \(\Sigma_{a}\) \cite{stratton1941}.

A finite aperture can support only phase oscillations whose transverse wavelength exceeds the local sampling
scale imposed by the aperture diameter \cite{stratton1941}.
Angular orders requiring finer lateral oscillations are converted into evanescent structure and therefore do not
contribute appreciably to the radiated field in $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$ \cite{devaney2004}.
This is the angular analogue of diffraction-limited imaging: Fourier optics, diffraction theory, antenna aperture
theory, and angular-momentum listing describe the same bandlimit from different coordinates
\cite{stratton1941}.
Finite-support geometric limitations unify lateral and spherical cuts in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch444}.
\subsection{Finite-support geometric limitations}
\label{sec:ch444}
% HARRINGTON-OPEN:sec:ch444
A Yagi array aimed at a fixed receive horn cannot sharpen nulls below the lateral bandlimit set by boom length and $\lambda$, no matter how many director elements are added \cite{harrington2001}. On finite support, pattern synthesis is bandlimited by geometry before any harmonic fit routine is opened \cite{stratton1941}. Maxwell's retarded export on the declared array support remains the governing constraint \cite{maxwell1873}.
The preceding subsections expressed the same obstruction in plane-wave, propagating, and spherical coordinates \cite{hansen1988}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch444} collects those cuts into a single admissible spectral body $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\eta,\varepsilon)$ inside the realizable set $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})$ introduced in Section~\ref{sec:ch401} \cite{stratton1941}. Recall that $\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}$ is the admissible impressed-current class whose image under $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ defines $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})$---not every row in a method-of-moments mesh is physically drivable \cite{harrington2001}. Accessibility envelope $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}(\mathcal{C})$ adds a second, independent ceiling from finite probe count $N$ on the measurement configuration \cite{devaney2004}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch444
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch444-b2

Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch444} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}

Consider Yagi array aimed at a fixed receive horn. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Finite-support spectra forbid arbitrarily sharp nulls without enlarging support . 
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch444} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
On declared compact support $(\Omega_s,k_0)$, $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}$ requires that a target harmonic vector respect tail budget $\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}$, lateral bandlimit $k_{t,\max}(\eta)$, and radiative rank $r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)$ on declared compact support $(\Omega_s,k_0)$ \cite{stratton1941}. Laboratory success requires the three-way intersection of synthesis, geometry, and measurement---not membership in a complete chart $\mathbb{C}^{I}$ alone \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{def:ch4.finite-support-restriction}
The \emph{finite-support geometric limitation} \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\eta,\varepsilon)\subset\mathbb{C}^{I}\) is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.finite-support-restriction}
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}
=
\left\{
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C}):
\|\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}\le\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}},\
k_{t}(\mathbf{c})\le k_{t,\max}(\eta),\
\operatorname{rank}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{c})\le r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)
\right\},
\end{equation}
where \(k_{t}(\mathbf{c})\) is the maximum transverse wavenumber with appreciable weight in the plane-wave transform of
\(\mathbf{c}\) and \(\operatorname{rank}_{\varepsilon}\) counts singular directions above threshold
\cite{hansen1988}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.finite-support-restriction} is the admissible spectral body inside realizability: synthesis
targets must lie in \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\), not merely in \(\mathbb{C}^{I}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
In aligned gauges,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.restriction-inclusion}
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}
\subseteq
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})
\subseteq
\mathbb{C}^{I},
\end{equation}
with strict inclusions under compact \(\Omega_{s}\) \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.restriction-gauge-equivalence}
For ball support \(B_{a}\), tolerance pair \((\eta,\varepsilon)\), and VSH/plane-wave listings related by
Section~\ref{sec:ch38}, the cuts \((\ell_{\max},k_{t,\max},r_{\mathrm{rad}})\) are equivalent up to constants
\(C_{1},C_{2}\) depending only on \((\eta,\varepsilon,k_{0}a)\):
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.gauge-equivalence}
\ell_{\max}\sim k_{t,\max}/k_{0}
\sim
r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
(\ref{prop:ch4.geometry-spectral-equivalence}), (\ref{thm:ch4.duality-bandlimit}), and
(\ref{def:ch4.radiative-rank}) with basis transformation boundedness from
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.basis-change-matrix} \cite{hansen1988}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.restriction-gauge-equivalence}) is the capstone of Section~\ref{sec:ch44}: bandlimits are not
representation accidents---they are the same finite-support constraint in different coordinates
\cite{devaney2004}. Design in plane-wave gauges must satisfy \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) after
transforming to the laboratory \(\mathcal{C}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
Laboratory targets must therefore lie in the intersection of what synthesis can drive and what finite instruments can recover:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.realizable-accessible-intersection}
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}(\mathcal{C}),
\end{equation}
which may be strictly smaller than either set alone \cite{devaney2004}.
Gauge changes and restriction to subdomains alter coefficient phase conventions but not flux-normalized power on $\Gamma_R$ when meters are consistent \cite{belinfante1940}. Two laboratories reporting different $\ell$ headlines for the same pattern often differ only in gauge or chart, not in physics \cite{hansen1988}.

Geometry compresses synthesis through \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\), while
instruments compress recovery through \(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.restriction-gauge-equivalence}) guarantees that the three cuts inside
\(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) are redundant readings of one obstruction once \((\eta,\varepsilon,k_{0}a)\) are fixed
\cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.gauge-redundant-check}
If \(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\) fails any one of the inequalities in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.finite-support-restriction} on \(B_{a}\) at tolerance \((\eta,\varepsilon)\), then it fails
\(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) and lies outside the realizable-accessible intersection
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.realizable-accessible-intersection} for every equivalent gauge related by
(\ref{thm:ch4.restriction-gauge-equivalence}) \cite{hansen1988}.
Define the verification functional
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.unified-truncation-functional}
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fs}}[\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}]
=
\max\Big\{
\frac{\|\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}}{\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}},
\frac{k_{t}(\mathbf{c})}{k_{t,\max}(\eta)},
\frac{\operatorname{rank}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{c})}{r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)},
\delta_{\mathrm{prop}}
\Big\},
\end{equation}
with \(\delta_{\mathrm{prop}}=0\) iff Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-realizable} holds and
\(\delta_{\mathrm{prop}}=1\) otherwise \cite{harrington2001}. Then
\(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\iff\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fs}}\le1\) and
\(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) requires additionally
\(\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0\) from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.synthesis-residual-norm} \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.finite-support certificate}
For compact \(B_{a}\), flux-normalized \(\mathcal{C}\), and tolerances \((\eta,\varepsilon)\),
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) if and only if
\(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fs}}[\mathbf{c}]\le1\) and \(\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0\) \cite{hansen1988}.
(\ref{def:ch4.finite-support-restriction}), (\ref{thm:ch4.restriction-gauge-equivalence}), and
(\ref{prin:ch4.operator-composition}) \cite{hansen1988}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.finite-support certificate}) is the operational summary of Section~\ref{sec:ch44}: four inequalities in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.finite-support-restriction} collapse to one verification functional plus a range residual
\cite{devaney2004}. Laboratories may implement \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) as a pre-flight check before MoM inversion
\cite{harrington2001}.
Laboratory success requires
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.certified-intersection-closure}
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{cert}}
\in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})
\cap
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}
\cap
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)},
\end{equation}
extending Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.realizable-accessible-intersection} \cite{harrington2001}. 
The finite-support synthesizable bound is the discipline an author applies before claiming range membership: quote $\Omega_s$, $k_0 D_s$, tolerance $\varepsilon$, and chart $\mathcal{C}$ together \cite{stratton1941}. Omitting support geometry invalidates rank and tail claims regardless of synthesis accuracy \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.three-way-certificate}
\(\mathbf{c}\) is laboratory-realizable and verifiable if and only if
\(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fs}}[\mathbf{c}]\le1\), \(\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0\), and
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}\) at declared instrument rank \(N\) \cite{hansen1988}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.finite-support certificate}), (\ref{def:ch4.accessibility-envelope}), and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.certified-intersection-closure} \cite{hansen1988}.
Reported patterns lie in the intersection of what synthesis can drive, what finite support permits, and what $N$ probes can resolve \cite{stratton1941}.
\section{Coupling to Angular-Momentum Eigenfields}
\label{sec:ch45}
Chapter~3 named the eigenfields; \ref{ch:04} asks which of them receive watts from bounded feeds and apertures \cite{stratton1941}. Coupling integrals are not post-processing weights but Maxwell projections of impressed currents onto flux-normalized templates---selection rules from symmetry are therefore hardware constraints, not index bookkeeping \cite{belinfante1940}. A pyramidal horn and a circular patch differ in which $(\ell,m,\sigma)$ blocks $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}$ excites even when the harmonic listing is complete \cite{collin2001}. Modal weights here presuppose $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ rank and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-certificate} \cite{harrington2001}.
Which multipole sectors receive watts from a given feed is decided by coupling integrals on $\Omega_s$, not by chart cardinality \cite{stratton1941}. Section~\ref{sec:ch45} weights \ref{ch:03} eigenfields through the actual source geometry \cite{harrington2001}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch45
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch45-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Circular patch on a compact ground-plane radiator]
A second bench observation matters on Helical feed on a satellite reflector. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch45} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Circular patch on a compact ground-plane radiator]
Consider a circular patch on a compact ground-plane radiator. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch45} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Rank is physical possibility, not numerical bookkeeping: it counts independent far-zone patterns finite geometry can populate \cite{harrington2001}. Bandlimits in Section~\ref{sec:ch44} restrict which patterns lie in $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}$; Section~\ref{sec:ch45} weights those patterns through multipole coupling on the actual feed geometry \cite{stratton1941}. A complete VSH chart lists channels; overlap integrals decide which channels receive watts from a $\lambda/10$ slot or offset reflector feed \cite{devaney2004}. Coupling coefficients are geometry filters on multipole radiation: only sectors whose spatial structure fits inside the source support receive appreciable excitation, regardless of chart cardinality \cite{stratton1941}.
\subsection{Eigenfield expansions of radiated fields}
\label{sec:ch451}
Multipole expansions organize far-zone power by angular order \cite{stratton1941}. A complete vector-spherical-harmonic chart is a dictionary; overlap integrals on the actual feed decide which dictionary entries receive watts. A $\lambda/10$ slot may list high-$\ell$ partners in a full multipole listing while coupling negligible power into those sectors---chart cardinality alone does not equal hardware channels \cite{chu1948}. Offset reflector feeds and dual-polarization horns display sector mixing on compact ranges without enlarging $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ \cite{harrington2001}.
\medskip
Once \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) has bounded the spectral body of synthesis, the remaining question is coordinate
resolution: in which angular-momentum eigenfields must \(\mathfrak{F}=\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp]\) be listed so that
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) is both synthesizable and verifiable
\cite{hansen1988}? Section~\ref{sec:ch34} fixed \(\mathbf{u}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) as the outgoing
harmonic pairs Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch3.VSH-M-pair}--\eqref{eq:ch3.Nlm-def}; Subsection~\ref{sec:ch451} reads those pairs as
the inevitable multipole basis on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) when \(\mathcal{C}\) is flux-normalized
\cite{stratton1941}.
Fix \(\omega>0\), outgoing \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\), \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) from
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch414}, and \(\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{adm}}\) with flux normalization
Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch3.VSH-unit-flux-normalization}--\eqref{eq:ch3.continuous-measure-flux-fix} from
Section~\ref{sec:ch35} \cite{stratton1941}. Use \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) of
(\ref{def:ch4.finite-support-restriction}) and radiative dimension \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) from
(\ref{def:ch4.radiative-rank}) \cite{harrington2001}.
for \(\mathfrak{F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\), the normalized expansion of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.normalized-coefficient-resolution} reads

\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.eigenfield-synthesis-expansion}
\mathfrak{F}
=
\sum_{\ell,m,\sigma}
\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\,
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)},
\qquad
\sigma\in\{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N}\},
\end{equation}
with \(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) the flux-normalized partners of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.VSH-M-pair} \cite{hansen1988}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.eigenfield-synthesis-expansion}
is not a new harmonic construction: it is the synthesis-side restatement of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.eigenfield-expansion-on-frad} on \(\operatorname{ran}\mathcal{R}_\omega\) after
\(\mathcal{N}\) has fixed the gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Coefficients are multipole coordinates:
A pyramidal horn at its mouth primarily couples to low-$m$ sectors in a flux-normalized VSH chart; higher
$\ell$ weights appear only as electrical size of the mouth grows \cite{collin2001}.
Coupling integrals therefore weight $\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}$ sparsely on $\operatorname{ran}\mathcal{R}_\omega$:
the eigenfield basis is complete in $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$, but finite geometry selects which labels receive
appreciable excitation \cite{stratton1941}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.gamma-eigenfield-coordinates}
\mathbf{c}
=
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]
=
\big\{
\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]
\big\}_{\ell,m,\sigma},
\qquad
\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]
=
\big\langle
\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp],\,
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}
\big\rangle_{\mathrm{rad}},
\end{equation}
using the pairing Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.eigenfield-coefficient-pairing} with \(\mathcal{C}\) identified as
\(\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{coef}}\) on the VSH sector \cite{hansen1988}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-eigenfield-coordinates} makes \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) a subset of
\(\mathbb{C}^{I_{\mathrm{VSH}}}\): realizability is a statement about achievable \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\), not
about the abstract existence of labels \cite{devaney2004}.
on spheres where (\ref{thm:ch2.far-field-approximation}) holds,
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.far-pattern-eigenfield-coupling} is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.far-pattern-eigenfield-sum}
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}[\Jimp](\hat{\mathbf{r}})
=
\sum_{\ell,m,\sigma}
\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]\,
\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}(\hat{\mathbf{r}}),
\end{equation}
with \(\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) the transverse far-zone profiles of
\(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) \cite{stratton1941}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.far-pattern-eigenfield-sum} is the pattern-space image of
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\): every synthesizable far-zone template is a finite superposition of eigenfield
shapes weighted by \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\)
\cite{hansen1988}. Targets whose \(\mathcal{A}_{\infty}\) requires eigenfield components outside
\(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) fail before coupling integrals are evaluated \cite{devaney2004}.
