\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) is not an abstract listing but the eigenfield coordinate map on
\(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) \cite{harrington2001}. Orthogonality
(\ref{thm:ch3.vsh-orthogonality}) makes distinct \((\ell,m,\sigma)\) labels power-orthogonal on
\(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) after \(\mathcal{N}\), so watt budgets decompose across eigenfield sectors in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.mode-power-from-coefficient} \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.eigenfield-synthesis-coordinates}
Let \(\mathcal{C}\) be the flux-normalized VSH listing of Section~\ref{sec:ch35}. For every
\(\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.synthesis-set-eigenfield}
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})
=
\left\{
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{I_{\mathrm{VSH}}}:
\mathbf{c}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]\ \text{for some}\ \Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}
\right\}
\subseteq
\operatorname{span}\big\{
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}:\,
(\ell,m,\sigma)\ \text{within}\ \mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}
\big\},
\end{equation}
with strict inclusion when compact \(\Omega_{s}\) compresses the accessible index set below the formal listing
\cite{stratton1941}. Moreover, power satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.eigenfield-power-decomposition}
\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}^{2}
=
\sum_{\ell,m,\sigma}
\big|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\big|^{2}
\end{equation}
in the aligned flux gauge of (\ref{thm:ch3.vsh-unit-flux-diagonal}) \cite{hansen1988}.
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.synthesis-set-eigenfield} is (\ref{def:ch4.realizable-synthesis-set}) with
\(\mathcal{C}\) specialized to Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-eigenfield-coordinates} \cite{stratton1941}. Inclusion in the
span of accessible eigenfields follows from \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) and compactness of
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) (\ref{thm:ch4.compact-radiation-map}) \cite{harrington2001}.
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.eigenfield-power-decomposition} is Parseval under
(\ref{thm:ch3.vsh-orthogonality}) and flux normalization
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.VSH-unit-flux-normalization} \cite{hansen1988}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.eigenfield-synthesis-coordinates}) elevates the VSH listing from representation convenience to
synthesis geometry: \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) is the image of \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) in the
eigenfield coordinate chart, and \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) selects which coordinates may carry appreciable weight
\cite{hansen1988}. Laboratories that report \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) without specifying
Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch3.VSH-unit-flux-normalization}--\eqref{eq:ch3.continuous-measure-flux-fix} compare coordinates in
different gauges, not different physics \cite{harrington2001}.
Integral route consistency. (\ref{thm:ch3.expansion-consistency}) guarantees that eigenfield coefficients computed by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-eigenfield-coordinates} agree with the modal sum of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch2.radiation-map-modal-sum} once indices are aligned by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.spectral-index-alignment} \cite{stratton1941}. The synthesis verification therefore commutes:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.integral-eigenfield-commute}
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]
=
\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp]
=
\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{I}_\omega[\Jimp]
\quad\text{on}\ \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{I}_\omega\) the integral route of Subsection~\ref{sec:ch251} \cite{harrington2001}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.integral-eigenfield-commute} prevents a common chain error: evaluating coupling integrals on raw
\(\mathcal{S}_\omega[\Jimp]\) before \(\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\) mixes reactive content into \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\)
\cite{stratton1941}.
Eigenfield coordinates are the natural multipole language of a spherical near-field range \cite{hansen1988}. Synthesis in $\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}$ is synthesis in watt-weighted angular sectors once flux normalization from Chapter~\ref{ch:03} is declared \cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.truncated-eigenfield-synthesis}
Let \(\mathcal{T}_{\ell_{\max}}\) be Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.ellmax-truncation-projector}. Then
\(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}^{(\ell_{\max})}(\mathcal{C})
=
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{(\ell_{\max})}[\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}]\)
and
\(\|\mathbf{c}-\mathbf{c}^{(\ell_{\max})}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}
\le
\|\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}\)
with remainder \(\mathbf{R}_{\ell_{\max}}\) from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.truncation-remainder-energy}
\cite{hansen1988}.
Corollary~\ref{cor:ch4.truncated-eigenfield-synthesis} links eigenfield listing to truncated set of patterns a source can radiate of
(\ref{prop:ch4.compressed-synthesis}): raising \(\ell_{\max}\) refines \(\mathbf{c}\) monotonically but cannot
excavate sectors forbidden by \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
Superposition on \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\). Linearity of \(\mathcal{R}_\omega\) and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.representation-linearity} give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.eigenfield-superposition}
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\big[a{\Jimp}_{1}+b{\Jimp}_{2}\big]
=
a\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[{\Jimp}_{1}]
b\,\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[{\Jimp}_{2}]
\end{equation}
for \(a,b\in\mathbb{C}\) and \({\Jimp}_{1},{\Jimp}_{2}\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.eigenfield-superposition} is the synthesis-side linearity law: eigenfield weights add in
\(\mathbf{c}\) because radiation is linear in impressed sources on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Nonlinearity enters only through boundary classes, material response, or inversion---not through the eigenfield listing
itself \cite{harrington2001}.
The discrete mom reduction proceeds as follows: on a patch mesh, Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.mom-synthesis-matrix} becomes
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.mom-eigenfield-matrix}
\mathbf{c}
=
\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{VSH}}\,\mathbf{I}_{s},
\qquad
(T_{\mathrm{VSH}})_{(\ell m\sigma),p}
=
\big\langle
\mathcal{R}_\omega[\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{p}],\,
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}
\big\rangle_{\mathrm{rad}},
\end{equation}
with Rao--Wilton--Glisson bases \(\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{p}\}\) \cite{harrington2001}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.mom-eigenfield-matrix} is the discrete form of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-eigenfield-coordinates}: each row
is an eigenfield coupling to a patch current \cite{stratton1941}. Rank of \(\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{VSH}}\) is bounded by
\(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) and Corollary~\ref{cor:ch4.channel-count} \cite{harrington2001}.
on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\), \(\mathbf{M}\) and \(\mathbf{N}\) sectors couple through the dyadic structure of
\(\mathcal{R}_\omega\) even when \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\mathbf{M})}\) and \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\mathbf{N})}\) are
listed separately \cite{stratton1941}. Define partial couplings
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.sector-partial-coupling}
\gamma_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]
=
\big\langle
\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp],\,
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}
\big\rangle_{\mathrm{rad}},
\qquad
\sigma\in\{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N}\},
\end{equation}
and the normalized sector coupling matrix
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.sector-coupling-matrix}
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma\sigma'}^{(\ell m)}[\Jimp]
=
\frac{
\gamma_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]\,
\overline{\gamma_{\ell m}^{(\sigma')}[\Jimp]}
}{
\|\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp]\|_{\mathrm{rad}}^{2}
},
\end{equation}
for \(\sigma,\sigma'\in\{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N}\}\) \cite{hansen1988}. On isotropic compact feeds,
\(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{MN}}^{(\ell m)}\approx0\) for \(\ell=1\); on chiral or offset feeds, sector mixing is mandatory in
synthesis templates \cite{jackson1999}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.sector-diagonal-dominance}
For \(k_{0}D_{s}\ll1\) and centered \(\Omega_{s}\), \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) is well approximated by
the \(\ell=1\) sector-diagonal subspace spanned by \(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{1m}^{(\mathbf{M})}\) and
\(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{1m}^{(\mathbf{N})}\) with negligible \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{MN}}^{(1m)}\)
\cite{jackson1999}. Targets with large cross-sector weight at \(\ell=1\) require broken
symmetry in \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
\subsection{Coupling integrals and selection rules}
\label{sec:ch452}
Chapter~3 constructed flux-normalized eigenfields $\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}$ on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$; Section~\ref{sec:ch45} now asks which of those fields receive watts from a given impressed current $\Jimp$ on $\Omega_s$ \cite{stratton1941}. The answer is not read from chart cardinality: coupling integrals on the actual feed geometry weight each sector before harmonic optimization begins \cite{harrington2001}. A dual-polarization horn may redistribute power among $(\ell,m,\sigma)$ labels; it does not enlarge $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ on fixed outline \cite{collin2001}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch452
\begin{physicalvignette}[Phased array panel on a positioner]
A phased array panel on a positioner in an outdoor far-field range illustrates coupling selection without new export channels. Offset feed lines and broken symmetry redistribute weight among multipole sectors visible on the turntable cuts, yet the electrical size of the panel fixes the same rank ceiling whether the beam is steered electronically or measured on a tower \cite{harrington2001}. Element count is not mode count \cite{devaney2004}.
When technicians steer the main lobe ten degrees electronically, the pattern moves as expected, but the sidelobe floor tied to finite support does not disappear \cite{stratton1941}. Steering reweights accessible directions; it does not create directions geometry never coupled \cite{pal2026}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch452} develops the coupling integrals that this array scene makes visible in measured decibels \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Selection rules---parity decoupling on centrosymmetric feeds, azimuthal block structure on bodies of revolution, singular-value thresholds on $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$---are physically imposed filters, not optional sparse priors for inversion procedures \cite{jackson1999}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch452} defines the modal coupling functional $\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]$, assembles the coupling matrix $\mathbf{K}$, and records the selection conditions under which $\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}$ must vanish on declared support \cite{hansen1988}.
Modal coupling integral. Define the \emph{modal coupling functional} by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.modal-coupling-integral}
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]
=
\int_{\Omega_{s}}
\Jimp(\mathbf{r})\cdot
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)\,\dagger}(\mathbf{r})\,
d^{3}r,
\end{equation}
where \(\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)\,\dagger}\) is the adjoint source mode dual to
\(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) under Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch2.radiative-coupling-bilinear}
\cite{stratton1941}. In the far-zone regime,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.coupling-far-zone-reduction}
\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]
=
\mathcal{Z}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\,
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp],
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{Z}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) absorbing propagation constants and flux normalization from
Section~\ref{sec:ch35} \cite{hansen1988}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-far-zone-reduction} expresses Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.far-pattern-eigenfield-coupling} in export language: each coefficient is a coupling integral,
not a fit parameter \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{def:ch4.modal-coupling-matrix}
For a finite index set \(I_{\mathrm{acc}}\subset\{(\ell,m,\sigma)\}\) within \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\), the
\emph{modal coupling matrix} is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.modal-coupling-matrix}
\mathbf{K}
=
\big[K_{(\ell m\sigma),(\ell'm'\sigma')}\big],
\qquad
K_{\alpha\beta}
=
\big\langle
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\alpha},\,
\mathcal{O}\,\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\beta}
\big\rangle_{\mathrm{rad}},
\end{equation}
for observation operator \(\mathcal{O}\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) and multi-indices
\(\alpha,\beta\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}\) \cite{harrington2001}. The synthesis operator satisfies
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]=\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{src}}\,\mathbf{j}\) when \(\mathbf{j}\) is the coefficient
vector of \(\Jimp\) in a declared source basis \cite{devaney2004}.
\(\mathbf{K}\) is the bilinear report of how eigenfield sectors talk to one another under passive
observation: diagonal dominance signals sparse synthesizable patterns; off-diagonal weight signals mandatory multi-mode
superposition \cite{hansen1988}. Reciprocity on lossless media forces
\(K_{\alpha\beta}=K_{\beta\alpha}\) as in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.reciprocity-coupling-symmetry} \cite{stratton1941}.
When \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) is invariant under a subgroup \(G\subset\mathrm{SO}(3)\), coupling integrals
vanish unless representation labels compose consistently \cite{jackson1999}. For full rotational symmetry on
isotropic \(\Omega_{s}\), Wigner mixing Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.eigenfield-Wigner-mixing} implies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.selection-delta-ell}
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]
\ \text{supported on}\ 
\ell\le\ell_{\max}(k_{0}D_{s}),
\end{equation}
from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} as a selection bound, and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.selection-parity}
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\mathbf{M})}\ \text{and}\ \mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\mathbf{N})}\ \text{decouple}
\quad\text{when}\ \mathfrak{E}\ \text{preserves parity},
\end{equation}
on centrosymmetric feeds \cite{stratton1941}. Equations~\eqref{eq:ch4.selection-delta-ell}--\eqref{eq:ch4.selection-parity}
are \emph{coupling selection rules}: they shrink the set of nonzero couplings before MoM discretization
\cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.coupling-selection-rules}
Let \(\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) on compact \(\Omega_{s}\) with \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}=1\). Then
\(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]=0\) whenever any of the following holds:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.selection-rules-list}
(\ell,m,\sigma)\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}},
\qquad
\sigma\ \text{forbidden by parity of }\mathfrak{E},
\qquad
|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|
<
\varepsilon\,\|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]\|
\ \text{with}\ \sigma_{\mathrm{rank}}<\varepsilon\sigma_{1},
\end{equation}
where the third condition is the singular-value selection rule from
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD} \cite{stratton1941}.
First condition: (\ref{def:ch4.finite-support-restriction}) \cite{hansen1988}. Second: degeneracy and parity
structure of Subsection~\ref{sec:ch345} applied to \(\mathfrak{E}\) \cite{jackson1999}. Third:
Eckart--Young truncation of \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) at \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\)
(\ref{thm:ch4.radiative-rank}) \cite{harrington2001}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.coupling-selection-rules}) is the inevitability statement of Subsection~\ref{sec:ch452}: selection is
not an optional sparse prior but the physically imposed consequence of compact support, symmetry, and radiative dimension
\cite{devaney2004}. Pattern synthesis schemes that activate forbidden \((\ell,m,\sigma)\) sectors optimize outside
\(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) even when MoM matrices are formally invertible \cite{harrington2001}.
Selection rules express which multipole sectors receive power from a given source symmetry---axisymmetric feeds excite primarily $m=0$ blocks, offset feeds mix $m$ \cite{jackson1999}. They prevent assigning $\ell=7$ content to a short dipole without enlarging electrical size \cite{harrington2001}.
\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\ChOneFigBlock{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[ch1fig, node distance=5mm and 7mm]
 \node[ch1box] (j) {$\Jimp$};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of j] (k) {Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.modal-coupling-integral}};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of k] (sel) {Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.selection-rules-list}};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of sel] (c) {$\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}$};
 \draw[ch1flow] (j) -- (k) -- (sel) -- (c);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\caption{Subsection~\ref{sec:ch452}: coupling integrals map sources to eigenfield weights; selection rules zero
forbidden sectors before synthesis.}
\label{fig:ch4.coupling-selection-chain}
\end{figure}
When products of angular sectors form composite labels, Clebsch--Gordan weights
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.CG-coupling-def} govern which bilinear couplings may appear in
\(\mathbf{K}\) \cite{jackson1999}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch452} cites those weights
addition-theorem proofs: the selection content is that coupling integrals inherit the same triangular
\((\ell_1,\ell_2,\ell_{3})\) admissibility as scalar harmonic products \cite{hansen1988}. Composite
orbital-mode claims must therefore be checked against both Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.selection-rules-list} and CG-admissible coupling
topology \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.so3-block-sparsity}
If \(\mathfrak{E}\) is rotationally symmetric on isotropic \(\Omega_{s}\), then \(\mathbf{K}\) is block-diagonal in
\((\ell,\sigma)\) up to \(m\)-mixing inside each \(\ell\) block through
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.eigenfield-Wigner-mixing} \cite{jackson1999}. 
The surface and aperture reduction proceeds as follows: when \(\Jimp\) is replaced by \(\mathbf{J}_{s}\) on \(\Sigma_{a}\) per Section~\ref{sec:ch42},
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.modal-coupling-integral} reduces to
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.aperture-coupling-integral}
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\mathbf{J}_{s}]
=
\int_{\Sigma_{a}}
\mathbf{J}_{s}(\mathbf{r})\cdot
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)\,\dagger}(\mathbf{r})\,
dS,
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{D}_{\tau}=1\) and \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}=1\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.aperture-coupling-integral} is how horn and slot designs enter eigenfield coordinates: aperture
currents are integrated against adjoint modes, not against unconstrained \(\E_{t}\) templates \cite{collin2001}.
The dual modes \(\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)\,\dagger}\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.modal-coupling-integral} are
fixed by the radiation adjoint of Subsection~\ref{sec:ch282}:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.adjoint-source-mode}
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)\,\dagger}
=
\mathcal{R}_\omega^{\dagger}\big[\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\big]
\quad\text{on}\ \Omega_{s},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{R}_\omega^{\dagger}\) the adjoint induced by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch2.radiative-coupling-bilinear} \cite{stratton1941}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.adjoint-source-mode} makes coupling integrals bilinear verification tests: changing \(\mathcal{C}\) rotates
\(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) and therefore rotates \(\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)\,\dagger}\)
consistently \cite{hansen1988}. Mismatch between transmit and receive adjoint modes breaks reciprocity symmetry in
\(\mathbf{K}\) without changing \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) \cite{harrington2001}.
on patches \(\{S_{p}\}\), assemble
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.discrete-coupling-matrix}
K_{\alpha p}
=
\int_{S_{p}}
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{p}(\mathbf{r})\cdot
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{r})\,dS,
\qquad
\mathbf{c}
=
\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{disc}}\,\mathbf{I}_{s},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathbf{I}_{s}\) patch currents \cite{harrington2001}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.discrete-coupling-matrix}
is the aperture specialization of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.mom-eigenfield-matrix}: selection rules appear as numerically
small rows of \(\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{disc}}\) before inversion \cite{stratton1941}. Rows below
\(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) should be zeroed by (\ref{thm:ch4.coupling-selection-rules}), not by arbitrary
Tikhonov priors \cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.sparse-coupling-matrix}
Under isotropic \(\mathrm{SO}(3)\) symmetry on \(B_{a}\), \(\mathbf{K}\) is block-sparse in \((\ell,\sigma)\) up to
numerical leakage \(\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mach}})\) when \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}=1\)
\cite{jackson1999}. Sparsity is a consequence of symmetry and compactness, not an optional
compressive prior \cite{devaney2004}.
On bodies with azimuthal symmetry about \(\hat{\mathbf{z}}\), coupling rows with \(|m|>m_{\max}(k_{0}D_{s})\) fall below
\(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD} \cite{jackson1999}. Parity selection
requires
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.parity-selection}
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]=0
\quad\text{when}\quad
\mathcal{P}\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}
=
-\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}
\ \text{and}\ 
\mathcal{P}\Jimp=\Jimp,
\end{equation}
for symmetry operator \(\mathcal{P}\) of the feed \cite{hansen1988}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.parity-selection} is the synthesis reading of Wigner \(D\)-matrix block sparsity from
Section~\ref{sec:ch36} \cite{jackson1999}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.symmetry-coupling-nulls}
If \(\mathcal{G}\) is a symmetry group of \((\Omega_{s},\Jimp)\) and \(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) transforms by
irrep \(\chi_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\), then \(\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]=0\) for every sector whose irrep does not
appear in the decomposition of \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) under \(\mathcal{G}\) \cite{hansen1988}.
Schur orthogonality on coupling integrals Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.modal-coupling-integral} \cite{jackson1999}.
Symmetry-induced nulls in coupling integrals are why certain multipoles never receive feed power on symmetric apertures \cite{stratton1941}. Breaking symmetry---offset feed, serrated rim, radome---fills those nulls without creating new radial propagation \cite{hansen1988}.
\subsection{Dependence on geometry and aperture size}
\label{sec:ch453}
Coupling integrals acquire their realizability content only after support outline and frequency are fixed \cite{stratton1941}. Enlarging dish diameter $D_s$ or raising $k_0$ at fixed mechanical outline activates higher-$\ell$ sectors because electrical size $\Xi=k_0 D_s/(2\pi)$ grows; a $\lambda/10$ slot remains a low-$\ell$ radiator until the opening itself is enlarged, not merely over-meshed \cite{chu1948}. Rim diffraction and offset feeds redistribute watts among admissible sectors; they do not create propagating channels forbidden by compact support on unchanged metal \cite{devaney2004}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch453
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch453-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Anechoic turntable with azimuth-over-elevation stage]
A second bench observation matters on Radar feed horn illuminating a corner reflector. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch453} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Anechoic turntable with azimuth-over-elevation stage]
Consider Anechoic turntable with azimuth-over-elevation stage. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Offset and truncation redistribute multipole weight without increasing electrical size . 
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch453} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
The geometry-weighted coupling operator $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(\Omega_s)$ collects all sector overlaps $\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]$ in one map from $\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}$ to the accessible index set $I_{\mathrm{acc}}\subset I$ inside $\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}$ \cite{harrington2001}. In the aligned flux gauge, $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega=\mathbf{Z}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}$: radiative dimension $r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)$ and effective rank $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ are operator properties of that composition, not of patch count on $\Sigma_a$ \cite{hansen1988}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch453} states scaling laws in volume and area form, separates near-field from far-zone coupling, and records the electrical-size crossover from dipole-class $\mathcal{O}(1)$ rank to mesoscale $(k_0 D_s)^2$ and large-body $(k_0 D_s)^3$ growth \cite{harrington2001}.
Geometry-weighted coupling operator. Define the \emph{geometry-weighted coupling operator} by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.geometry-coupling-operator}
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}(\Omega_{s})
:
\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}(\Omega_{s})
\longrightarrow
\mathbb{C}^{I_{\mathrm{acc}}},
\qquad
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}[\Jimp]
=
\big\{\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]\big\}_{(\ell,m,\sigma)\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.modal-coupling-integral} \cite{stratton1941}.
Composition with normalization gives \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega=\mathbf{Z}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\) for
diagonal \(\mathbf{Z}\) in the aligned flux gauge \cite{hansen1988}. Operator norm
\(\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\) grows with \(\operatorname{vol}(\Omega_{s})\) and
\(k_{0}D_{s}\) through Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.HS-bound} \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling}
For ball support \(B_{a}\) with \(k_{0}a\ge1\) and tolerance \(\varepsilon\in(0,1)\), the number of eigenfield
couplings above threshold satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling}
\#\big\{(\ell,m,\sigma):\ |\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]|\ge\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}[\Jimp]\|\big\}
\ \le\
C\,(k_{0}a)^{3},
\end{equation}
with \(C\) depending only on \(\varepsilon\) and flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. For aperture
\(\Sigma_{a}\) of area \(A_{a}\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.aperture-coupling-scaling}
\operatorname{rank}\mathbf{K}\big|_{\Sigma_{a}}
\ \le\
C'\,k_{0}^{2}A_{a},
\end{equation}
linking patch rank to electrical area \cite{collin2001}.
Inequality Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling} combines
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.weyl-mode-count} with radiative dimension (\ref{thm:ch4.radiative-rank})
\cite{stratton1941}. Aperture bound Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.aperture-coupling-scaling} follows from
MoM rank Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.mom-eigenfield-matrix} and standard aperture sampling limits
\cite{harrington2001}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling}) makes rank fixed by geometry and frequency explicit in the coupling formulation: enlarging
\(a\) or \(A_{a}\) increases the count of potentially strong eigenfield couplings, but fixed geometry caps that count at
fixed \((k_{0},\varepsilon)\) \cite{devaney2004}. Phase-only retuning on fixed \(\Sigma_{a}\) rotates
couplings within the same rank class; it does not raise \(\operatorname{rank}\mathbf{K}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
for \(\ell>k_{0}D_{s}\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.coupling-ell-decay}
\sup_{\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}}
\frac{|\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]|}
{\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}[\Jimp]\|}
\le
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell,k_{0}D_{s})\,
\ell^{-1}\exp(-\ell/k_{0}D_{s}),
\end{equation}
from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} at the coupling integral \cite{hansen1988}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-ell-decay} is the physical meaning of spectral compression for coupling design: high-\(\ell\)
channels are not merely weak in power---their coupling integrals are exponentially suppressed on compact support
\cite{harrington2001}.
Coupling strength scales with electrical size and aperture overlap integrals \cite{stratton1941}. Enlarging dish diameter or frequency raises coupling to high-$\ell$ sectors before phase-only surfaces are consulted \cite{harrington2001}.

 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of geom] (k0) {$k_{0}$};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of k0] (rank) {Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling}};

Figure~\ref{fig:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling} connects Section~\ref{sec:ch44} bandlimits to coupling engineering:
\(k_{t,\max}(\eta)\) and \(\ell_{\max}\) bound spectral support; Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling}--\eqref{eq:ch4.aperture-coupling-scaling}
bound how many couplings can be strong enough to matter at tolerance \(\varepsilon\) \cite{hansen1988}.
Corollary~\ref{cor:ch4.support-monotone-enlargement} is the set inclusion version of the same fact
\cite{stratton1941}.
Near-field versus far-zone coupling. Coupling integrals evaluated in the reactive shell may include evanescent weight absent from
\(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{stratton1941}. Denote by
\(\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{near})}\) the near evaluation and by
\(\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{far})}\) the far-zone reduction
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-far-zone-reduction} \cite{devaney2004}. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.near-far-coupling-split}
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{near})}
=
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{far})}
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{ev})},
\qquad
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\ \text{depends only on}\ 
\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{far})},
\end{equation}
by (\ref{prop:ch4.evanescent-invisibility}) \cite{stratton1941}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.near-far-coupling-split} is the coupling form of the propagating-sector restriction
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-realizable}: near-field scans overweight
\(\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{ev})}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.far-coupling-synthesis}
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) if and only if the far-zone coupling vector
\(\{\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma,\mathrm{far})}[\Jimp]\}\) lies in \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\)
at fixed \((\Omega_{s},\omega)\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Truncation shoulders are measured on $\Gamma_R$; declare $\varepsilon$ with every $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ headline \cite{poynting1884}.
Volume \(\Jimp\) on \(\Omega_{s}\) and aperture \(\mathbf{J}_{s}\) on \(\Sigma_{a}\) produce identical far-zone
\(\mathbf{c}\) when equivalence (\ref{thm:ch4.volume-surface-equivalence}) holds, but
\(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\) has different discrete rank on \(\Omega_{s}\) versus \(\Sigma_{a}\)
\cite{collin2001}. MoM on \(\Sigma_{a}\) yields
\(\operatorname{rank}\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{VSH}}\le P\) patches; volume discretizations may expose additional synthesis
directions at the same \(\mathbf{c}\) when containment fails \cite{devaney2004}.
Define the coupling ladder
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.coupling-ladder}
\mathcal{L}_{n}
=
\big\{(\ell,m,\sigma):\ \ell\le n,\ s_{\alpha}\ge\varepsilon\big\},
\qquad
\mathcal{L}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{n+1},
\end{equation}
with strict inclusion under Corollary~\ref{cor:ch4.support-monotone-enlargement} when \(D_{s}\) increases
\cite{stratton1941}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-ladder} orders design freedom by electrical
size: at fixed \(\omega\), enlarging \(\Omega_{s}\) activates higher rungs of the ladder; at fixed \(\Omega_{s}\), raising
\(\omega\) activates higher \(\ell\) before aperture measurement compresses further \cite{devaney2004}.
Phase-only synthesis on fixed hardware explores only \(\mathcal{L}_{n}\) at fixed \(n\), not \(\mathcal{L}_{n+k}\)
\cite{stratton1941}.
For prolate supports with aspect ratio \(\chi>1\), coupling rank along the major axis exceeds that along the minor axis
at the same \(\operatorname{vol}(\Omega_{s})\) \cite{stratton1941}. The geometry operator
\(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\) is not isotropic in \(\ell,m\): aligned \(\ell\) sectors may activate before
azimuthal partners with the same \(\ell\) \cite{harrington2001}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling}
remains a conservative isotropic bound; elongated feeds require directionally resolved verifications of \(\mathbf{K}\)
\cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{lem:ch4.coupling-freq-monotone}
At fixed \(\Omega_{s}\), \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) and the cardinality of \(\mathcal{L}_{n}\) are
non-decreasing in \(k_{0}\) until propagation cutoffs or material dispersion alter \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Frequency synthesis therefore enlarges coupling rank monotonically on fixed
geometry \cite{harrington2001}.
Coupling rank crosses three regimes as \(k_{0}D_{s}\) increases:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.coupling-crossover}
\underbrace{r_{\mathrm{rad}}\sim\mathcal{O}(1)}_{\text{Rayleigh}}
\ \longrightarrow\
\underbrace{r_{\mathrm{rad}}\sim(k_{0}D_{s})^{2}}_{\text{mesoscale}}
\ \longrightarrow\
\underbrace{r_{\mathrm{rad}}\sim(k_{0}D_{s})^{3}}_{\text{large-body}},
\end{equation}
from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.geometry-coupling-scaling} and standard aperture limits
\cite{stratton1941}. Inverse design must declare which regime the hardware occupies:
phase-only templates in the Rayleigh regime cannot access mesoscale rank without enlarging \(D_{s}\) or raising \(k_{0}\)
\cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.crossover-monotone}
At fixed \(\Omega_{s}\), \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) and \(\#\mathcal{L}_{n}\) are non-decreasing in \(k_{0}D_{s}\) until
material dispersion or superconducting cutoffs alter \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Enlarging \(D_{s}\) at fixed \(k_{0}\) produces the same monotonicity by
Corollary~\ref{cor:ch4.support-monotone-enlargement} \cite{harrington2001}.
\subsection{Modal weights and coupling strength}
\label{sec:ch454}
Coupling strength $s_\alpha$ reports how strongly sector $\alpha=(\ell,m,\sigma)$ is wired to the feed geometry; modal weight $w_\alpha=|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|^2$ reports how many watts that sector actually carries on the declared flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. The distinction matters in every superdirective controversy: a sector may be weakly coupled yet assigned large formal weight in a synthesis trial, or strongly coupled yet starved by feed passivity---only the intersection of coupling admissibility $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}=1$, feed ceiling $\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha;\mathfrak{E})$, and radiative rank $r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)$ defines achievable weights \cite{harrington2001}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch454
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch454-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Radio telescope dish with Cassegrain feed]
A second bench observation matters on Half-space ground plane with image currents. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch454} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Radio telescope dish with Cassegrain feed]
Consider Radio telescope dish with Cassegrain feed. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Modal weights must be read with the normalization and gauge declared in the prior chapter . 
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch454} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Physically, modal weights live in a convex feasible set bounded by passivity and support, not in an unconstrained orthant of $\mathbb{C}^{I}$ \cite{stratton1941}. Phase-only synthesis on fixed hardware explores rotations inside $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$; unreachable rows remain null regardless of synthesis iterations \cite{devaney2004}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch454} separates coupling strength from power-limited weight, states the weight ceiling, and closes with the feasible weight polytope $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{w}}$ that pattern-synthesis targets must respect \cite{hansen1988}.
Modal weight and coupling strength. Define the \emph{modal weight} as \(w_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}=|\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|^{2}\) and the
\emph{coupling strength} to sector \(\alpha=(\ell,m,\sigma)\) as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.coupling-strength-def}
s_{\alpha}
=
\frac{
|K_{\alpha\alpha}|
}{
\max_{\beta\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}}|K_{\beta\beta}|
},
\qquad
w_{\alpha}
\le
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha;\mathfrak{E}),
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}\) is determined by feed power and passivity \cite{stratton1941}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-strength-def} separates \emph{coupling set by geometry} \(s_{\alpha}\) from
\emph{power-limited weight} \(w_{\alpha}\): a sector may be strongly coupled yet weakly excited, or weakly coupled yet
dominant in a superdirective template that violates \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{def:ch4.modal-weight-ceiling}
For declared feed tuple \(\mathfrak{E}\) with available complex power \(P_{\mathrm{feed}}\), the \emph{modal weight
ceiling} is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.modal-weight-ceiling}
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha;\mathfrak{E})
=
\sup\big\{
w_{\alpha}:
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C}),\ 
\sum_{\beta}w_{\beta}\le P_{\mathrm{feed}}
\big\}.
\end{equation}
(\ref{def:ch4.modal-weight-ceiling}) coins the realizability bound on per-mode power reports: no
\(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) may claim more than \(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}\) watts in the flux gauge without
violating passivity or feed capability \cite{stratton1941}. The ceiling is independent of measurement rank;
\(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}\) may further shrink recoverable weights without changing
\(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
Normalization propagation to weights. Under gauge change \(\widehat{c}_{\alpha}\to\widehat{c}_{\alpha}/a_{\alpha}\),
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.coupling-normalization-propagation} gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.weight-gauge-transform}
w_{\alpha}'=\frac{w_{\alpha}}{a_{\alpha}^{2}},
\qquad
s_{\alpha\beta}'=\frac{s_{\alpha\beta}}{a_{\alpha}a_{\beta}},
\end{equation}
while \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) remains invariant under Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.power-budget-invariance}
\cite{hansen1988}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.weight-gauge-transform} is the verification rule for
comparing modal weights across codes: mismatched normalization inflates or deflates \(w_{\alpha}\) without changing
exported watts \cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.coupling-weight-ceiling}
If \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}=0\) on the discretized source, then \(\mathbf{c}\notin\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\)
even when formal MoM inversion returns coefficients \cite{harrington2001}. If \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}=1\)
and \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{real}}=1\), then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.weight-ceiling-bound}
w_{\alpha}
\le
\min\Big\{
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha;\mathfrak{E}),\ 
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{rank}}(\alpha;\varepsilon)
\Big\},
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{rank}}(\alpha;\varepsilon)=0\) when \(\sigma_{\alpha}<\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD} \cite{devaney2004}.
Coupling inadmissibility implies \(\mathbf{T}\) is not Fredholm-stable, so \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) has no stable
preimage \cite{harrington2001}. Passivity gives the power ceiling; singular-value threshold gives the radiative dimension from
(\ref{thm:ch4.radiative-rank}) \cite{stratton1941}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.coupling-weight-ceiling}) closes the coupling arc: \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}\),
\(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}\), and \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}\) are independent conditions whose intersection defines achievable
modal weights \cite{hansen1988}. Superdirective templates that concentrate \(w_{\alpha}\) in sectors with
\(s_{\alpha}\ll1\) typically violate Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-realizable} or \(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{fs}}\)
\cite{devaney2004}.
Each sector's coupling magnitude is bounded by geometry-set export gain $s_\alpha$ from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-strength-def} \cite{harrington2001}. Optimization cannot drive a sector above that ceiling without changing $\Omega_s$ or $\omega$ \cite{stratton1941}.

\centering
\ChOneFigBlock{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[ch1fig, node distance=5mm and 7mm]
 \node[ch1box] (cpl) {$\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}$};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of cpl] (w) {$\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}$};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of w] (r) {$r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)$};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of r] (wa) {$w_{\alpha}$};
 \draw[ch1flow] (cpl.east) -- (cpl.east |- wa.west);
 \draw[ch1flow] (cpl.east |- wa.west) -- (wa.west);
 \draw[ch1flow] (w.east) -- (w.east |- wa.west);
 \draw[ch1flow] (w.east |- wa.west) -- (wa.west);
 \draw[ch1flow] (r.east) -- (r.east |- wa.west);
 \draw[ch1flow] (r.east |- wa.west) -- (wa.west);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
\caption{Subsection~\ref{sec:ch454}: modal weight \(w_{\alpha}\) is bounded by coupling $\mathcal{D}_J=1$, feed ceiling, and
radiative dimension.}
\label{fig:ch4.modal-weight-ceiling}
\end{figure}
For a realizable superposition \(\mathbf{c}=\sum_{\alpha\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}}a_{\alpha}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}\),
passivity and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.eigenfield-power-decomposition} require
The laboratory situation is a bounded source at fixed $\omega$ observed on $\Gamma_R$ with declared flux gauge. The relation below connects that situation to the coefficient description inherited from Chapter~3. \cite{stratton1941}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.superposition-power-budget}
\sum_{\alpha\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}}|a_{\alpha}|^{2}
\le
P_{\mathrm{feed}},
\qquad
w_{\alpha}=|a_{\alpha}|^{2}\ \text{in flux gauge},
\end{equation}
with equality only when all power is exported through \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.superposition-power-budget} is the weight-space image of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.passivity-screen}: no combination of modal weights may exceed declared feed capability
\cite{harrington2001}. High-\(Q\) resonators store reactive energy that increases near-field couplings without raising
far-zone \(w_{\alpha}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.coupling-weight-ordering}
If \(s_{\alpha_{2}}\ll s_{\alpha_{1}}\) and \(w_{\alpha_{2}}\ge w_{\alpha_{1}}\) with
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\), then necessarily
\(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{2}}^{(\mathrm{ev})}\) carries appreciable reactive storage and
\(\widehat{c}_{\alpha_{2}}\) is not determined by far-zone data alone \cite{harrington2001}.
On reciprocal media, transmit and receive weights obey
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.reciprocity-coupling-symmetry}: asymmetry in reported \(w_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(tx)}}\) versus
\(w_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(rx)}}\) signals normalization mismatch, not new synthesizable power \cite{stratton1941}.
Reciprocity pairs sectors; it does not enlarge \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) beyond geometry rank
\cite{devaney2004}.
Accessibility versus synthesis ceilings. \(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}\) bounds synthesizable power per sector; \(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}\) bounds
recoverable coordinates at aperture rank \(N\) \cite{harrington2001}. A mode may satisfy
\(w_{\alpha}\le\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha)\) yet lie outside \(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}\) when the
receiver cannot resolve \(\alpha\) \cite{hansen1988}. Laboratory reports therefore require the intersection verification of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.realizable-accessible-intersection} applied to weights:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.weight-accessibility-intersection}
w_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{(cert)}}
\le
\min\big\{
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha),\ 
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}(\alpha)
\big\},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}\) the per-sector recovery ceiling induced by \(\mathcal{M}\)
\cite{devaney2004}.
Define the normalized coupling matrix \(\widetilde{K}_{\alpha\beta}=K_{\alpha\beta}/\sqrt{K_{\alpha\alpha}K_{\beta\beta}}\)
on \(I_{\mathrm{acc}}\) \cite{harrington2001}. Thresholding \(|\widetilde{K}_{\alpha\beta}|<\varepsilon\) defines a graph sparsity
pattern compatible with (\ref{thm:ch4.coupling-selection-rules}) \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.diagonal-dominance-rank}
If \(|\widetilde{K}_{\alpha\beta}|<\varepsilon\) for all \(\alpha\neq\beta\) on \(I_{\mathrm{acc}}\), then
\(\mathbf{c}\) is approximated within \(\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\) by a single-sector template at the maximizing
\(s_{\alpha}\) \cite{harrington2001}. Multi-sector templates with comparable weights require
off-diagonal coupling above threshold \cite{hansen1988}.
for sectors \(\alpha\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}\), passivity requires
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.multimode-power-closure}
\sum_{\alpha\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}}
w_{\alpha}
\le
P_{\mathrm{feed}},
\qquad
w_{\alpha}
=
|a_{\alpha}|^{2}
\ \text{in flux gauge},
\end{equation}
extending Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.superposition-power-budget} \cite{stratton1941}. Each
\(w_{\alpha}\) is additionally capped by \(\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha)\) and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-ell-decay} \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.feasible-weight-polytope}
The set of realizable weight vectors \(\mathbf{w}=(w_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in I_{\mathrm{acc}}}\) is a convex polytope
\(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|I_{\mathrm{acc}}|}\) cut by hyperplanes
\(w_{\alpha}\le\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{ceiling}}(\alpha)\), Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.multimode-power-closure}, and
\(w_{\alpha}=0\) for sectors nullified by (\ref{thm:ch4.coupling-selection-rules})
\cite{stratton1941}. A target \(\mathbf{w}_{\mathrm{target}}\notin\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{w}}\)
implies \(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\notin\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) regardless of phase optimization
\cite{hansen1988}.
Linearity of \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) and convexity of passivity constraints
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.passivity-screen} \cite{stratton1941}.
Feasible weight sets form a polytope in coefficient space: passivity and support confine which watt partitions are simultaneously realizable \cite{harrington2001}. Vendor claims of uniform excitation across all $\ell\le L$ often lie outside that polytope at fixed $k_0 a$ \cite{stratton1941}.
\section{Truncation and Effective Dimension}
\label{sec:ch46}
Truncation is the link between complete harmonic catalogs and finite hardware reports \cite{hansen1988}. The operative count is how many $\ell$ blocks carry power above a declared tolerance on $\Gamma_R$ for a source of diameter $D_s$ at wavenumber $k_0$ \cite{chu1948}. An enlarged harmonic listing at high $\ell_{\max}$ may look conservative in numerical synthesis; on the outgoing branch, tail watts in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch3.truncation-remainder-energy} are often geometry-forbidden rather than missing export \cite{stratton1941}. The subsections below translate tail decay into supported angular order, scaling laws, and the effective electromagnetic dimension $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ that laboratories report alongside gain and pattern tables \cite{harrington2001}.
Truncation is the visible laboratory consequence of the rank limits established in Sections~\ref{sec:ch43}--\ref{sec:ch45}: maximum supported order $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_0 a)$, tail budget $\eta_{\mathrm{tail}}$, and effective electromagnetic dimension $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ are three coordinate readings of the same geometry-imposed ceiling on $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ \cite{chu1948}. \ref{ch:04} asks which patterns finite hardware can generate; truncation names the highest angular orders and radiative channels that answer still exceeds laboratory tolerance $\varepsilon$ on declared support \cite{devaney2004}. Effective dimension is earned by support diameter and frequency---not assigned by harmonic listing length, not created by optimization on fixed outline \cite{stratton1941}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch46
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch46-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Rectangular horn mouth in a ground-plane flange]
A second bench observation matters on Open-ended coax probe above a calibration table. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch46} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Rectangular horn mouth in a ground-plane flange]
Consider Rectangular horn mouth in a ground-plane flange. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch46} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Multipole coupling explains \emph{which} $\ell$ sectors receive power from a given feed; Section~\ref{sec:ch46} names the highest order that still carries export above tolerance $\varepsilon$ and the integer that counts watt-distinguishable far-zone patterns on $\Gamma_R$ \cite{hansen1988}. Truncation is therefore a measured phenomenon---tail energy in discarded sectors, singular-value cliffs on the synthesis operator---before it is reduced to a synthesis truncation parameter or an arbitrarily enlarged harmonic listing for $\ell_{\max}$ \cite{harrington2001}. The subsections below replace informal truncation by verifiable objects: $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}$, joint volume--area scaling exponents, coupling-tail compression, and the established integer $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ \cite{jackson1999}.
\subsection{Maximum supported angular order}
\label{sec:ch461}
The maximum supported angular order $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_0 a)$ is the highest $\ell$ block whose aligned singular value on $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ still exceeds $\varepsilon\sigma_{1}$ on declared compact support---not the largest index visible in a solver truncation list \cite{hansen1988}. Electrical size $k_0 a$ sets the ladder; tolerance $\varepsilon$ sets the watt floor on $\Gamma_R$; both must accompany every published $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}$ report \cite{chu1948}. A synthesis truncation parameter for $\ell_{\max}$ does not override geometry: tail sectors below the range noise floor may remain unpopulated even when synthesis assigns chart coefficients without export above tolerance \cite{devaney2004}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch461
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch461-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Rotating scatterer on a lighthouse-style beam chopper]
A second bench observation matters on Dual-polarization horn pair on a compact range. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch461} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Rotating scatterer on a lighthouse-style beam chopper]
Consider Rotating scatterer on a lighthouse-style beam chopper. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Maximum supported $\ell$ is the highest angular order that still carries export power above tolerance on the declared support \cite{hansen1988}. 
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch461} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Before quoting orbital-mode headlines, integrate tail watts above $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}$ on the measurement sphere; padding rows inflate harmonic charts without enlarging wattmeter readings \cite{poynting1884}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch461} defines $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}$ through Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD}, states scaling $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\sim k_0 a+\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ on $B_a$, and gives the MoM verification protocol that separates synthesis-active rows from padding \cite{hansen1988}.
Truncation begins as a watt question on $\Gamma_R$: which $\ell$ blocks carry power above $\varepsilon\sigma_{1}$ on the declared support \cite{hansen1988}? Harmonic truncation limits and enlarged listing length $\ell_{\max}$ are conveniences layered on top of that measurement; they do not define $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}$ \cite{chu1948}. Laboratories that publish $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}$ beside $\ell_{\max}$ prevent coordinate inflation in synthesis reports \cite{jackson1999}.
The subsection develops the monotone chain of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-supp-monotone}: enlarging $k_0 a$ opens higher sectors; tightening $\varepsilon$ lowers the established ceiling \cite{hansen1988}. Every later scaling law in Section~\ref{sec:ch46} inherits this integer as the angular cutoff established here \cite{stratton1941}.
Boundary quantization in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch372} replaces continuous direction spectra by countable mode sets under
\(\Gamma_{b}\) \cite{stratton1941}. Phase retuning relabels harmonics: any \(\ell_{\max}\) may be
declared in \(\mathcal{C}\). Realizability is rank fixed by geometry and frequency: only sectors coupled through \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) above
\(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) participate in \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Fix ball support \(B_{a}\) with radius \(a\), flux-normalized VSH listing \(\mathcal{C}\), and tolerance \(\varepsilon\in(0,1)\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Use Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay}, (\ref{def:ch4.radiative-rank}), and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD} \cite{harrington2001}. Narrowband phasors \(\exp(-\jj\omega t)\) and SI units are in force
\cite{jackson1999}. Synthesis verifications use \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega=\mathbf{Z}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\) in the aligned
flux gauge of Section~\ref{sec:ch34} \cite{hansen1988}.
Define the \emph{maximum supported angular order}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-max-supported}
\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)
=
\max\left\{
\ell:\ \exists\,(\ell,m,\sigma)\ \text{with}\ 
\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}\ge\varepsilon\,\sigma_{1}
\ \text{in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD}}
\right\},
\end{equation}
where \(\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}\) is the singular value of \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) aligned to the unit export vector
\(\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) in the VSH chart \cite{harrington2001}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-max-supported}
is the synthesis-side angular cutoff: modes with \(\ell>\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) lie below export threshold at \(\varepsilon\) regardless of
listing labels or plotting resolution \cite{stratton1941}. \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) is the largest block index in
the singular-value ordering whose leading value still exceeds \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\). In physical terms, it is the highest azimuthal order that
can receive appreciable watts from any admissible \(\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}(B_{a})\) at the declared frequency
\cite{hansen1988}.
Electric and magnetic sectors need not share the same ceiling on anisotropic feeds \cite{harrington2001}. Define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-sector-supported}
\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}^{(\sigma)}(\varepsilon)
=
\max\big\{\ell:\ \exists m\ \text{with}\ \sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}\ge\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\big\},
\qquad
\sigma\in\{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N}\},
\end{equation}
and aggrecriterion \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon)=\max_{\sigma}\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}^{(\sigma)}(\varepsilon)\) \cite{hansen1988}.
Sector splitting precedes combined orbital-mode claims: a dominant \(\mathbf{N}\)-sector at \(\ell=5\) may coexist with a suppressed
\(\mathbf{M}\)-sector at the same \(\ell\) on offset feeds \cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.ell-max-scaling}
For compact \(B_{a}\) and aligned VSH gauge,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-max-scaling}
\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)
\ \le\
\ell_{\max}(\eta_{\mathrm{tail}},k_{0}a)
\ \sim\
k_{0}a+\mathcal{O}\!\big(\log(1/\varepsilon)\big),
\end{equation}
from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ellmax-from-tail} and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} \cite{hansen1988}.
Equality holds in the isotropic ball case up to gauge constants \cite{harrington2001}.
Tail envelope Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} bounds nonzero \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\) by
\(\exp(-\ell/k_{0}a)\) on \(B_{a}\) \cite{hansen1988}. Singular-value ordering of \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) on VSH
sectors identifies the largest \(\ell\) whose aligned \(\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}\) remains above \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Combining the tail bound with (\ref{def:ch4.radiative-rank}) yields
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-max-scaling} \cite{devaney2004}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.ell-max-scaling}) states that electrical size $k_{0}a$ fixes the highest angular order that can carry export power above tolerance \cite{jackson1999}. For the source, impressed currents on a ball of radius $a$ cannot populate $\ell\gg k_{0}a$ sectors. For the aperture, dish diameter sets the $\ell$ ladder before feed design. In the far zone, high-$\ell$ tail sectors decay exponentially in coefficient space. 
(\ref{thm:ch4.ell-max-scaling}) is the open-region counterpart of Subsection~\ref{sec:ch372} quantization: geometry imposes a
countable ceiling on \(\ell\), not merely on mesh indices \cite{stratton1941}. Phase-only metasurfaces on fixed \(a\) cannot raise
\(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) without raising \(k_{0}a\) or enlarging \(\mathcal{L}_{2}\) support \cite{devaney2004}. 
Electrical radius $k_0 a$ sets the highest $\ell$ that can carry export above noise floor $\varepsilon$ \cite{jackson1999}. Electrically small apertures cannot host dominant $\ell=10$ content; enlarging the dish or band does \cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.listing-vs-supported}
If \(\ell_{\max}>\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)\), indices \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}<\ell\le\ell_{\max}\) are export-padding at
tolerance \(\varepsilon\) \cite{hansen1988}. MoM inversion must not treat those rows as independent synthesis
coordinates \cite{harrington2001}.

 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of ell] (svd) {Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD}};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of svd] (sr) {$\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$};

\end{figure}
Tail-to-threshold translation. Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.high-ell-decay} implies that sector power at \(\ell\) is suppressed by \(\exp(-2\ell/k_{0}a)\) relative to low-\(\ell\)
sectors after flux normalization \cite{hansen1988}. Requiring \(\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}\ge\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) therefore
forces \(\ell\lesssim k_{0}a+\log(1/\varepsilon)\) \cite{stratton1941}. At \(\varepsilon=10^{-2}\) and \(k_{0}a=2\), the logarithmic
correction adds only \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) to the linear term: \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) is dominated by electrical size, not by numerical tolerance alone
\cite{harrington2001}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-supp-monotone}
\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)}{\partial(k_{0}a)}\ge0,\qquad
\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)}{\partial\varepsilon}\le0.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-supp-monotone} is the monotone chain used in laboratory verifications: enlarging the ball or raising frequency opens
higher \(\ell\) sectors; tightening \(\varepsilon\) lowers \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) \cite{hansen1988}.
As a worked example, at \(\varepsilon=10^{-2}\), representative centered-feed values are \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\approx3,5,7,9\) for
\(k_{0}a=1,1.5,2,2.5\) \cite{hansen1988}. Any design target with \(\ell>\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(k_{0}a)\) is outside
\(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) on \(B_{a}\) before MoM or pattern synthesis begins \cite{stratton1941}.
Gauge invariance of \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) requires that admissible changes of \(\mathcal{C}\) that preserve flux normalization and \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) leave \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\)
invariant \cite{hansen1988}. Changing \(\ell_{\max}\) without changing \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) adds null rows to
\(\mathbf{T}\) but does not enlarge \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) \cite{harrington2001}. Laboratories must
report \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) alongside \(\ell_{\max}\) to prevent coordinate inflation in synthesis reports \cite{hansen1988}.
The relation to \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) is that each supported \(\ell\) block contributes up to \((2\ell+1)\) sector labels, but \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) counts only singular
directions above \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) \cite{harrington2001}. Thus \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) is necessary but not sufficient
for rank saturation: within \(\ell\le\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\), many \((m,\sigma)\) pairs may still lie below threshold
\cite{hansen1988}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-deff-relation}
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\le\sum_{\ell=0}^{\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}}(2\ell+1)\times\#\{\sigma:\sigma\text{ active}\},
\end{equation}
with equality only when every sector in the sum exceeds \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
The MoM row tolerance protocol begins as follows. Let \(\mathbf{T}\) be the discretized radiation map in the aligned gauge \cite{harrington2001}. Row \( (\ell,m,\sigma)\) is \emph{synthesis-active}
if \(\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}\ge\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\); otherwise it is \emph{padding} per Corollary~\ref{cor:ch4.listing-vs-supported}
\cite{hansen1988}. The verification protocol is: (i) form \(\mathbf{T}\) on \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}(B_{a})\); (ii) extract
\(\{\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}\}\); (iii) compute \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) via Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-max-supported}; (iv) truncate templates to
active rows only \cite{stratton1941}. 
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.padding-invisible}
Rows with \(\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}<\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) carry no component of \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) above
threshold: for any \(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\), the coefficient on padding row \((\ell,m,\sigma)\) is
\(\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\|\mathbf{c}\|\) in the flux gauge \cite{harrington2001}.
Eckart--Young truncation at rank \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) discards singular directions below \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\)
\cite{harrington2001}. Padding rows span those directions \cite{hansen1988}.
The VSH chart is complete on \(S^{2}\): any square-integrable far pattern admits an expansion in \(\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}\)
\cite{hansen1988}. Completeness is a representation theorem; \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) is a equivalence theorem on
\(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}(B_{a})\) \cite{harrington2001}. Confusing the two is the root error in orbital-mode device claims that
display high-\(\ell\) coefficients produced by numerical noise or gauge padding \cite{jackson1999}. Export checks must always ask:
does \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}]\) carry weight above \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) in that sector?
\cite{stratton1941}.
Define block participation
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-block-participation}
\mathrm{PPR}(\ell)=\frac{\sum_{m,\sigma}\sigma_{(\ell,m,\sigma)}^{2}}{\sum_{\ell',m',\sigma'}\sigma_{(\ell',m',\sigma')}^{2}},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathrm{PPR}(\ell)<\varepsilon^{2}\) for all \(\ell>\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) in the aligned gauge \cite{harrington2001}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-block-participation} converts singular-value verifications into per-order power language laboratories already use
\cite{devaney2004}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ell-supp-certificate}
\underbrace{\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)}_{\text{angular ceiling}}
\ \le\
\underbrace{\ell_{\max}(\eta_{\mathrm{tail}},k_{0}a)}_{\text{tail bound}}
\ \le\
\underbrace{k_{0}a+\mathcal{O}(\log(1/\varepsilon))}_{\text{Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-max-scaling}}}.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-supp-certificate} is the bound sequence for synthesis specifications \cite{hansen1988}.
Padding invisible indices are chart rows whose singular values fall below $\varepsilon\sigma_1$: they appear in analysis listings but carry no export above tolerance \cite{hansen1988}. Retaining $\ell\le 15$ in a MoM model when $\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\le 8$ inflates matrix size without enlarging hardware capability \cite{harrington2001}.

\subsection{Scaling laws for spectral truncation}
\label{sec:ch462}
Scaling laws translate electrical size into growth exponents the laboratory can verify before trusting a rank headline \cite{stratton1941}. Enclosed feeds and volumetric supports on ball $B_a$ inherit mode-budget growth $\sim (k_0 a)^3$ in radiative dimension $r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)$; mouth-only apertures and flat panels on area $A_a$ inherit $\sim k_0^2 A_a$ \cite{harrington2001}. Comparing a compact planar radiator report to a reflector dish report without declaring support class compares unlike budgets---the operative exponent follows $\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}$, not the longest harmonic listing \cite{devaney2004}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch462
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch462-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Waveguide TE10 mouth in a flange radiating to half-space]
A second bench observation matters on Microstrip patch array on a finite substrate. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch462} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Waveguide TE10 mouth in a flange radiating to half-space]
Consider Waveguide TE10 mouth in a flange radiating to half-space. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Scaling laws tell how rank grows with frequency and volume . They are guides for design budgets, not targets to exceed without enlarging hardware .
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch462} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Scaling laws bound singular-value decay and retained rank; they state how steeply singular values of $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ should fall and how many directions should remain above $\varepsilon\sigma_{1}$ at fixed $(\Omega_s,k_0)$ \cite{hansen1988}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch462} records volume and area forms, the joint bound $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\le\min\{C_V(k_0 a)^3,C_A k_0^2 A_a\}$, and the frequency ladder laboratories must recompute when band changes at fixed mechanical outline \cite{jackson1999}.
on enclosing ball \(B_{a}\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.truncation-volume-scaling}
r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)
\ \sim\
C_{\mathrm{vol}}(\varepsilon)\,(k_{0}a)^{3},
\qquad
\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)
\ \sim\
C_{\ell}(\varepsilon)\,k_{0}a,
\end{equation}
from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.weyl-mode-count} and (\ref{thm:ch4.ell-max-scaling}) \cite{stratton1941}. The cubic
exponent on \(r_{\mathrm{rad}}\) is the export-side image of volume mode density: each additional wavelength inside \(B_{a}\) opens new
orthogonal radiation channels \cite{harrington2001}. The linear exponent on \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) is the angular tail counterpart: high
orders are permitted only in proportion to \(k_{0}a\) \cite{hansen1988}. On aperture \(\Sigma_{a}\) of area \(A_{a}\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.truncation-area-scaling}
\operatorname{rank}\mathbf{K}\big|_{\Sigma_{a}}
\ \sim\
C_{A}(\varepsilon)\,k_{0}^{2}A_{a},
\end{equation}
from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.aperture-coupling-scaling} \cite{collin2001}. Volume scaling dominates when synthesis is
declared on the enclosing ball; area scaling governs mouth-only and patch-array models \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.truncation-scaling-consistency}
If equivalence (\ref{thm:ch4.volume-surface-equivalence}) holds and \(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{cpl}}=1\),
Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-volume-scaling} and \eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-area-scaling} bound the same \(\mathbf{c}\) image up to
constants depending on \(\varepsilon\) and mesh alignment \cite{stratton1941}. Neither exponent is raised by phase-only retuning on fixed geometry \cite{devaney2004}.
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) depends on \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) support class; phase rotations preserve singular values on fixed
support \cite{harrington2001}. Volume--surface equivalence identifies images up to equivalence constants \cite{collin2001}.
Joint scaling consistency check. Laboratories should report a single conservative bound
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.joint-scaling-law}
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\le\min\big\{C_{V}(\varepsilon)(k_{0}a)^{3},\,C_{A}(\varepsilon)k_{0}^{2}A_{a}\big\},
\end{equation}
with the active term determined by declared synthesis support \cite{stratton1941}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.joint-scaling-law}
prevents aperture-only optimism when currents occupy a larger enclosing volume \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.scaling-gauge-invariant}
Exponents \(3\) and \(2\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.joint-scaling-law} are invariant under admissible changes of \(\mathcal{C}\); only
prefactors \(C_{V},C_{A}\) depend on flux normalization \cite{hansen1988}.
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) is unitarily equivalent under flux gauge changes; singular-value thresholds scale homogeneously, leaving rank
growth exponents unchanged \cite{hansen1988}.
Scaling laws for mode count and rank are gauge-invariant once flux normalization is fixed \cite{stratton1941}. Comparing coefficients across independent analysis codes without declared meters invalidates scaling comparisons \cite{jackson1999}.

 \draw[ch1flow] (vol.east |- min.west) -- (min.west);

 \draw[ch1flow] (area.east |- min.west) -- (min.west);

}

\label{fig:ch4.truncation-scaling-verification}
