Changing mechanical outline without updating electrical size $k_{0}D_{s}$ leaves rank claims inconsistent across frequency bands \cite{jackson1999}.
Prefactor sensitivity. Constants \(C_{V}(\varepsilon)\) and \(C_{A}(\varepsilon)\) depend on \(\varepsilon\) and gauge but not on \(\ell_{\max}\)
\cite{hansen1988}. On a circular aperture,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.scaling-ratio}
\frac{C_{V}(\varepsilon)(k_{0}a)^{3}}{C_{A}(\varepsilon)k_{0}^{2}A_{a}}
\sim
\frac{C_{V}}{C_{A}}\cdot\frac{k_{0}a}{\pi}.
\end{equation}
At \(k_{0}a=3\) with \(C_{V}/C_{A}\approx1\), volume and area bounds are comparable: both must be verified \cite{harrington2001}.
Panel with \(k_{0}W=3\) gives \(k_{0}^{2}A_{a}\sim9\pi\approx28\) and \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(10^{-2})\lesssim30\) before element-count claims
\cite{hansen1988}.
when \(k_{0}a\ll1\), Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-volume-scaling} is superseded by dipole-class rank \(\le3\) \cite{jackson1999}.
Scaling laws must name the active regime before extrapolation \cite{stratton1941}.
Design frequency ladder. Hold \(\operatorname{diam}(\Omega_{s})\) fixed and sample \(k_{0}\) at bands \(f_{1}<f_{2}<f_{3}\). Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.freq-ladder}
\frac{d_{\mathrm{eff}}(f_{3})}{d_{\mathrm{eff}}(f_{1})}
\approx
\left(\frac{f_{3}}{f_{1}}\right)^{3}
\quad\text{on }B_{a},\qquad
\frac{\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(f_{3})}{\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(f_{1})}
\approx
\frac{f_{3}}{f_{1}},
\end{equation}
in the large-body regime \cite{jackson1999}. Compact-aperture design cycles that quote a single ``mode count'' across decades of
frequency without recomputing Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.freq-ladder} are not verifiable \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.outline-rank-noninvariant}
Mechanical outline invariance with frequency change leaves \(\operatorname{diam}(\Omega_{s})\) fixed but increases \(k_{0}D_{s}\), hence
increases \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) per Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-volume-scaling} \cite{jackson1999}.
Derivation sketch (volume exponent). Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-volume-scaling} inherits the Weyl counting exponent from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.weyl-mode-count} on \(B_{a}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Each wavelength cubed inside the ball adds \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) orthogonal radiation channels at fixed
\(\varepsilon\) because \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) is a compact operator whose essential rank tracks volume mode density
\cite{hansen1988}. The linear \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) exponent in the same equation pair is the angular shadow of
that volume law: high orders cannot participate until \(k_{0}a\) is large enough to defeat tail decay \cite{jackson1999}.
when \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) is surface-supported on \(\Sigma_{a}\), rank growth is quadratic in wavenumber times area because
tangential currents on a two-dimensional manifold carry a two-dimensional spatial mode budget \cite{collin2001}.
Patch arrays sample that budget; they do not create new budget beyond \(k_{0}^{2}A_{a}\) \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.exponent-selection}
Let \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(vol)}}\sim(k_{0}a)^{3}\) and \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(ap)}}\sim k_{0}^{2}A_{a}\). The operative
truncation exponent is determined by \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) support dimension: volume-fed synthesis uses exponent \(3\);
aperture-fed synthesis uses exponent \(2\) \cite{stratton1941}. Mixed models require explicit
domain declaration \cite{devaney2004}.
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) is defined on the declared \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) class; rank growth follows support Hausdorff
dimension through Weyl and sampling laws cited above \cite{stratton1941}.
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.weyl-mode-count} counts modes in a phase-space ball; Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-volume-scaling} counts radiative channels
above \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) \cite{stratton1941}. The connecting inequality
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.weyl-to-realizability}
r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\le C_{\mathrm{Weyl}}(\varepsilon)\,N_{\mathrm{mode}}^{\mathrm{(vol)}}
\le
C_{\mathrm{Weyl}}(\varepsilon)\,C_{V}(\varepsilon)(k_{0}a)^{3}
\end{equation}
states that $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ rank cannot exceed Weyl mode budget at the same tolerance \cite{harrington2001}. Philosophically,
Weyl counting is a necessary condition for export; sufficient strength requires \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) singular values
\cite{hansen1988}.
Tail exponent selection ties high-$\ell$ decay to support class---volume-like versus area-like enclosures \cite{hansen1988}.
\subsection{Decay of high-order coupling}
\label{sec:ch463}
% CH04-NARR-EXP:04.463
Coupling tails bound how strongly feed geometry can excite high-$\ell$ blocks even when a complete chart lists those rows \cite{hansen1988}. A sidelobe campaign that chases $\ell=10$ on a $\lambda/8$ outline therefore collides with the tail law before the feed network is optimized \cite{stratton1941}.
Spectral tail decay in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch443} bounded exported watts in high-$\ell$ blocks; the coupling-tail law bounds how strongly feed geometry can \emph{excite} those blocks through $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}$ even when a complete VSH chart lists every row \cite{hansen1988}. On compact support with diameter $D_s$, sectors with $\ell\gg k_0 D_s$ carry coupling integrals suppressed by $\ell^{-1}\exp(-\ell/k_0 D_s)$: the sidelobe campaign that chases $\ell=10$ on a $\lambda/8$ outline therefore collides with tail physics before the feed network is optimized \cite{chu1948}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch463
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch463-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Conical horn feeding a reflector edge]
A second bench observation matters on Planar near-field scanner above a compact planar test article. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch463} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Conical horn feeding a reflector edge]
Consider Conical horn feeding a reflector edge. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Coupling tails explain why high-order sectors remain weak even when synthesis fits assign them coefficients. 
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch463} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
The laboratory implication is a listing-versus-export diagnostic. Channel density of states grows $\sim \ell_{\max}^2$ with chart cutoff, but radiative dimension $r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)$ and coupling-tail mass $\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}})$ compress once $\ell_{\max}\gg k_0 D_s$ \cite{devaney2004}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch463} states the uniform coupling-tail envelope, links tail mass to $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$, and defines the listing inflation factor $\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{DOS}}$ that exposes padding rows MoM may mistake for physical channels \cite{hansen1988}.
for \(\ell>k_{0}D_{s}\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.coupling-tail-envelope}
\sup_{\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}}
\frac{|\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}[\Jimp]|}{\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}[\Jimp]\|}
\le
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{tail}}(\ell,k_{0}D_{s})\,
\ell^{-1}\exp(-\ell/k_{0}D_{s}),
\end{equation}
extending Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-ell-decay} with sector constants \(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{tail}}\)
\cite{hansen1988}. Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-tail-envelope} is a uniform operator bound on high-$\ell$ coupling blocks: sources on $\Omega_s$ cannot strongly excite angular orders far beyond electrical size \cite{harrington2001}.
Spectral tail decay and coupling-tail decay are two readings of the same geometry: the first counts exported watts in $\ell$ blocks; the second counts how strongly feed geometry can excite those blocks through $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}$ \cite{hansen1988}. When an enlarged harmonic listing with $\ell_{\max}\gg k_0 D_s$ is retained, coupling tails compress long before feed synthesis proceeds---sidelobe campaigns that target high $\ell$ on modest apertures fight the tail law, not merely the feed network \cite{chu1948}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.coupling-tail-compression}
If Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-tail-envelope} holds, then
\(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\le C(k_{0}D_{s},\varepsilon)\) with \(C\) growing slower than raw channel count \((2\ell_{\max}+1)^{2}\)
from Subsection~\ref{sec:ch353} \cite{hansen1988}. Listing size overstates export dimension when
\(\ell_{\max}\gg k_{0}D_{s}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
Summing Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-tail-envelope} over \((m,\sigma)\) yields exponentially small tail mass for \(\ell\gg k_{0}D_{s}\)
\cite{hansen1988}. Comparing summed mass to \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\) in (\ref{def:ch4.radiative-rank}) bounds
\(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) independently of \(\ell_{\max}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
High-order coupling blocks carry negligible export energy on compact supports: geometry $\to$ spectral compression $\to$ rank reduction \cite{hansen1988}. For the source, only $\mathcal{O}(k_{0}D_{s})$ angular orders couple strongly. In measurement, complete mode charts with $\ell_{\max}\gg k_{0}D_{s}$ overstate independent channels \cite{harrington2001}.
High-order coupling tails compress exponentially on compact support \cite{hansen1988}. Sidelobe engineering that targets $\ell\ge 8$ on $k_0 a<2$ hardware fights the tail law, not merely the feed synthesis \cite{stratton1941}.
Cumulative coupling tail mass. Define
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.coupling-tail-mass}
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}})=\sum_{\ell>\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}}(2\ell+1)\max_{m,\sigma}|\mathcal{K}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}|^{2}.
\end{equation}
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.coupling-tail-dos}
\(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}})\) decays exponentially for \(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}\gg k_{0}D_{s}\) while DOS grows as
\(\mathcal{O}(\ell_{\max}^{2})\) \cite{hansen1988}.
Tail mass integral bound. Integrating the envelope gives \(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}})\lesssim\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{tail}}\exp(-\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}/k_{0}D_{s})\)
\cite{hansen1988}. At \(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}=\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon)\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.tail-mass-bound}
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}})\le\varepsilon^{2}\sigma_{1}^{2},\qquad
\sum_{\ell\le\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}}(2\ell+1)\ge d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.tail-mass-bound} links angular cutoff to export threshold \cite{harrington2001}.

Spherical near-field facilities and radio-telescope receivers encounter probe-limited recovery \cite{hansen1988}.
At \(k_{0}D_{s}=1.5\), \(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}=8\) yields \(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}\lesssim10^{-2}\) of total coupling mass
\cite{hansen1988}.
Compression optimality link. Rank-\(r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)\) truncation is optimal for \(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\) in operator norm
\cite{harrington2001}. Retaining \(\ell_{\max}\gg\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) adds null directions that MoM may misinterpret as
coupled channels \cite{stratton1941}.
DOS inflation diagnostic. Define the \emph{listing inflation factor}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.dos-inflation}
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{DOS}}
=
\frac{(2\ell_{\max}+1)^{2}}{d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)}
\approx
\frac{\text{channel labels}}{\text{radiative channels}}.
\end{equation}
When \(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{DOS}}\gg1\), MoM practitioners are fitting noise in padding rows \cite{harrington2001}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.coupling-tail-compression}) explains why \(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{DOS}}\) grows with \(\ell_{\max}\) at fixed \(k_{0}D_{s}\)
\cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.safe-listing-width}
A conservative listing satisfies \(\ell_{\max}\le\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}D_{s})+\mathcal{O}(1)\) in the aligned gauge
\cite{hansen1988}. Wider listings do not enlarge \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch353} answers how many angular channels the sphere can label at cutoff \(\ell_{\max}\) \cite{jackson1999}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch463} answers how many of those labels \(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\) can excite from \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Designers who equate ``channel count'' with ``beam count'' use a representation measure into a power measure
\cite{hansen1988}. 
Spherical near-field facilities and radio-telescope receivers encounter probe-limited recovery \cite{hansen1988}.
Tail summation bound (explicit). Summing Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.coupling-tail-envelope} over \(\ell>\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}\) and using \(\sum_{\ell}(2\ell+1)\ell^{-2}\le\pi^{2}/6\)
gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.tail-summation-explicit}
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}})
\le
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{tail}}'\exp\!\bigl(-\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}/k_{0}D_{s}\bigr)\,\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}\|^{2},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{tail}}'\) depending only on sector normalization \cite{hansen1988}. At \(\ell_{\mathrm{cut}}=\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\),
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.tail-summation-explicit} establishes that discarded orders carry less than \(\varepsilon^{2}\) of peak coupling
\cite{harrington2001}.
Define realizability compression
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.realizability-compression}
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{real}}
=
\frac{d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)}{(2\ell_{\max}+1)^{2}}
\approx
\frac{\text{radiative channels}}{\text{listed channels}}.
\end{equation}
When \(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{real}}\ll1\), (\ref{thm:ch4.coupling-tail-compression}) has already fired: most channels are coupling-invisible
\cite{hansen1988}. MIMO specification that cites \((2\ell_{\max}+1)^{2}\) without \(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{real}}\)
conflates DOS with diversity \cite{jackson1999}.
\subsection{Effective rank and electromagnetic dimension}
\label{sec:ch464}
Effective electromagnetic dimension $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ is the integer a laboratory reads from the singular-value cliff on $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$: the number of independent far-zone patterns that carry power above $\varepsilon\sigma_{1}$ on the declared flux gauge \cite{harrington2001}. It is not patch count, not $\ell_{\max}$, and not the length of a harmonic listing---those are coordinate artifacts that may exceed watt-bearing diversity on compact hardware \cite{hansen1988}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch464
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch464-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Cylindrical near-field arch with probe carriage]
A second bench observation matters on Offset-fed reflector on a satellite bus. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch464} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Cylindrical near-field arch with probe carriage]
Consider Cylindrical near-field arch with probe carriage. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Effective dimension counts directions that matter in watts at declared tolerance . 
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch464} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
The tolerance procedure is deliberately mundane. Form $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ on declared support $\Omega_s$; read $\{\sigma_n\}$ from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD}; count directions with $\sigma_n\ge\varepsilon\sigma_1$; publish $(\varepsilon,\,k_0 D_s,\,d_{\mathrm{eff}},\,\mathcal{C}\text{ gauge})$ alongside any coefficient table \cite{hansen1988}. When MoM mesh rank exceeds continuum rank on fixed outline, conservative reporting uses $d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(report)}}=\min(d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(MoM)}},r_{\mathrm{rad}}^{\mathrm{(cont)}}(\varepsilon))$ per Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.deff-conservative} \cite{harrington2001}. Array spec sheets that cite element count without $(k_0 D_s,\varepsilon)$ confuse listing length with radiative rank \cite{devaney2004}.
High-order coupling tails and $d_{\mathrm{eff}}$ are two readings of one singular-value cliff \cite{hansen1988}. Coupling tails bound how strongly geometry excites $\ell$ blocks through $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{geo}}$; $d_{\mathrm{eff}}$ counts how many radiative channels exceed the same tolerance on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$ \cite{stratton1941}. Subsection~\ref{sec:ch464} develops Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.effective-dimension} to the Chapter~4 name for geometry-imposed export dimension and closes Section~\ref{sec:ch46} with the unified truncation bound Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-certificate} \cite{jackson1999}.
Use (\ref{def:ch4.radiative-rank}), Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD}, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.effective-dimension}
\cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{def:ch4.effective-em-dimension}
The \emph{effective electromagnetic dimension} of \((\Omega_{s},\omega,\mathcal{C})\) at tolerance \(\varepsilon\) is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.effective-em-dimension}
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)
=
r_{\mathrm{rad}}(\varepsilon)
=
\#\big\{n:\ \sigma_{n}\ge\varepsilon\,\sigma_{1}\big\},
\end{equation}
with \(\{\sigma_{n}\}\) from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.gamma-SVD} \cite{stratton1941}.
Effective electromagnetic dimension counts independent synthesis directions above a noise floor at declared tolerance: the operator measure of how many angular channels finite geometry leaves open---what a phased-array engineer would call usable degrees of freedom, derived here from $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ rather than from element count alone \cite{hansen1988}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.effective-em-dimension} develops Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.effective-dimension} to the \ref{ch:04}
name for geometry-imposed export dimension \cite{devaney2004}. \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) is a discrete counting functional on
the singular spectrum. In physical terms, it is the number of independent far-zone degrees of freedom a finite source can export at declared power
resolution \(\varepsilon\) \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.dimension-bounds-realizability}
\(\dim\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\le d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) at tolerance \(\varepsilon\), and every
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) has at most \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) independent components above export
threshold \cite{stratton1941}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.radiative-rank}) identifies \(\dim\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) with the radiative dimension above threshold
\cite{harrington2001}. (\ref{def:ch4.effective-em-dimension}) counts the same singular directions \cite{stratton1941}.
Reports must state \((\varepsilon,\,k_{0}D_{s},\,\mathcal{C}\text{ gauge})\) with \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\)
\cite{hansen1988}.
Electromagnetic dimension is radiative rank at fixed geometry, not chart cardinality \cite{harrington2001}. 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.deff-reporting}
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)=\#\{n:\sigma_{n}\ge\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\},\qquad
\Delta d_{\mathrm{eff}}=|d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(MoM)}}-d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(cont)}}|.
\end{equation}
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.deff-mesh-gap}
\(\Delta d_{\mathrm{eff}}\le\Delta r_{\mathrm{gap}}\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.rank-gap} \cite{harrington2001}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.deff-conservative}
d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(report)}}=\min\big(d_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{(MoM)}},\,r_{\mathrm{rad}}^{\mathrm{(cont)}}(\varepsilon)\big).
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.deff-conservative} is the conservative laboratory integer when mesh rank exceeds continuum rank
\cite{hansen1988}.

 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of svd] (rr) {$r_{\mathrm{rad}}$};
 \node[ch1box, right=7mm of rr] (deff) {$d_{\mathrm{eff}}$};

% MASTER-REV:fig-reading-fig-ch4-effective-dimension
Effective electromagnetic dimension $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ counts independent far-zone directions a finite source can excite above a noise floor---the operator expression of Chu--Harrington diversity limits on real hardware \cite{chu1948}. Listing width in VSH or plane-wave charts may exceed $d_{\mathrm{eff}}$; only singular values above threshold carry measurable watts \cite{stratton1941}.
\(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) \(\to\) realizable coefficient set \cite{hansen1988}.
\(k_{0}D_{s}\approx1\) at sub-6\,GHz gives \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(10^{-2})\le3\); same outline at \(28\,\mathrm{GHz}\) gives
\(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\sim\mathcal{O}(10^{1})\)--\(\mathcal{O}(10^{2})\) per Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.joint-scaling-law} \cite{jackson1999}.
Link to \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\). Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.ell-deff-relation} shows \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\le\sum_{\ell\le\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}}(2\ell+1)\times\#\{\text{active }\sigma\}\)
\cite{harrington2001}. Angular ceiling and integer dimension must be reported together \cite{hansen1988}.
Tolerance sweep bounds. Laboratories should report \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) as a function of \(\varepsilon\) on fixed \((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\):
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.deff-epsilon-sweep}
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon_{2})\le d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon_{1})\quad\text{for }\varepsilon_{2}\ge\varepsilon_{1},
\end{equation}
with \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(10^{-1})\) a coarse upper bound and \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(10^{-3})\) a fine verification \cite{hansen1988}.
A rank claim at unstated \(\varepsilon\) is not comparable across groups \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.deff-monotone-epsilon}
For fixed \((\Omega_{s},k_{0},\mathcal{C})\), \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) is non-increasing in \(\varepsilon\)
\cite{harrington2001}.
The index set \(\{n:\sigma_{n}\ge\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\}\) shrinks as \(\varepsilon\) increases \cite{harrington2001}.
\(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) is the point where geometry, frequency, and tolerance meet in a single verifiable integer
\cite{stratton1941}. It is not an artifact of MoM discretization if \(\Delta d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) is
reported; it is not a specification mode count; it is not \(\ell_{\max}\). 
Unified truncation bound. Collecting Subsections~\ref{sec:ch461}--\ref{sec:ch464}, a source \((\Omega_{s},k_{0},\mathcal{C},\varepsilon)\) satisfies the
\emph{truncation bound} when
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.truncation-certificate}
\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}D_{s})\ \text{defined},\quad
d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\le\min\{C_{V}(k_{0}a)^{3},C_{A}k_{0}^{2}A_{a}\},\quad
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cpl}}(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}})\le\varepsilon^{2}\sigma_{1}^{2},\quad
\Delta d_{\mathrm{eff}}\ \text{reported}.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.truncation-certificate} is the Section~\ref{sec:ch46} bound relations: angular ceiling, scaling bound, tail mass, and
mesh gap \cite{hansen1988}.
Subsections~\ref{sec:ch461}--\ref{sec:ch464} form a closed $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ arc on truncation. \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\) names the angular ceiling;
scaling exponents name how fast \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) grows with electrical size; coupling tails show why channel DOS overstates that growth;
\(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) is the laboratory integer that survives singular-value verification \cite{stratton1941}.
Effective rank $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ decreases as tolerance $\varepsilon$ tightens---fewer directions carry power above a higher floor \cite{harrington2001}. Laboratory reports must quote $\varepsilon$ alongside $d_{\mathrm{eff}}$ \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.truncation-inevitability}
On any compact \(\Omega_{s}\) at fixed \((k_{0},\varepsilon)\), \(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) is finite-dimensional with
dimension \(\le d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)<\infty\) \cite{stratton1941}. Infinite complete mode charts are
coordinate artifacts; export is always finite-rank \cite{jackson1999}.
the effective electromagnetic dimension $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ derived here is the physically imposed
expression of a physical principle that appears in different forms in the classical literature
\cite{harrington2001}.
Chu interpreted the same limitation through stored energy and $Q$ bounds on minimum-size antennas
\cite{chu1948}.
Harrington interpreted it through source degrees of freedom and MoM rank on finite bodies \cite{harrington2001}.
Landau and Pollak interpreted it through spectral concentration and prolate spheroidal modes
\cite{landau1962}.
The present formulation shows that these results arise from one finite-geometry restriction on
$\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ \cite{stratton1941}.
Section~\ref{sec:ch46} has replaced heuristic truncation by \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}\), joint volume--area scaling exponents, coupling-tail
compression, and the established integer \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) \cite{stratton1941}. Export
rank is now named in both angular-order and singular-value language; laboratories can verify synthesis reports without conflating harmonic listing cardinality
with watts \cite{hansen1988}. Section~\ref{sec:ch47} treats null spaces, reactive accessibility, and inversion limits on the same operator
chain \cite{stratton1941}.
\section{Null Spaces, $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}$, and Reconstruction}
\label{sec:ch47}
Synthesis rank and measurement rank are independent ceilings on the same export \cite{devaney2004}. Section~\ref{sec:ch47} develops the recovery side of \ref{ch:04}: non-radiating additions invisible to far patterns, reactive sheets that dense near-field scans resolve without enlarging $\operatorname{ran}\mathcal{R}_\omega$, and finite probe banks that saturate $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}$ before $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ is exhausted \cite{stratton1941}. Nullity of $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$---a synthesis quotient problem---must be distinguished from ill-conditioning of inversion on a fixed scan sphere---a measurement tolerance problem \cite{harrington2001}. Compact-range measurement recording often reports a single residual without separating NR nullity from probe-rank saturation \cite{hansen1988}.
Synthesis may succeed while reconstruction fails when $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}(\mathcal{C})$ is poorer than $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})$---the measurement story of \ref{ch:04} \cite{devaney2004}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch47
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch47-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Corporate-feed phased array behind a radome]
A second bench observation matters on Loop antenna for magnetic-dipole calibration. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch47} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Corporate-feed phased array behind a radome]
Consider Corporate-feed phased array behind a radome. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch47} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Radiative rank counts how many independent far-zone patterns a finite source can synthesize; measurement rank counts how many of those patterns a finite instrument can recover \cite{stratton1941}. The laboratory distinction is physical before it is algebraic: non-radiating currents alter the near field without changing the radiated coefficient vector $\mathbf{c}$; unreachable chart directions cannot be driven by any impressed current on the declared support; reactive evanescent storage must not be reported as far-zone diversity \cite{devaney2004}. Section~\ref{sec:ch47} develops these limits as consequences of compact support and finite observation, using the radiation definitions of \ref{ch:02}--\ref{ch:03} by reference only \cite{stratton1941}.
In a measurement chamber, null spaces mark structure---modes that exist in a complete harmonic listing yet remain invisible to far-zone meters, or visible near the feed yet absent from propagating export \cite{devaney2004}. Radiative rank is necessary for observability but never sufficient when probe count, standoff, or scan bandwidth compress recovery \cite{jackson1999}.
\subsection{Near-field scanning and probe-limited recovery}
\label{sec:ch47.signature-nfscan}
Scan spheres sample tangential fields at finite locations \cite{harrington2001}. Probe count caps recovery even when the source is rich: a feed that populates ten radiative channels above tolerance may still be measured on a sixty-probe scan that resolves only six on $\Gamma_R$ \cite{devaney2004}. The synthesis report and the recovery report must therefore publish separate integers---$d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ and probe-limited recovery rank---with the same $\varepsilon$ and flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Chapter~4 asks which patterns finite hardware can generate; here the limiting operator is often $\mathcal{M}_N$, not $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ \cite{harrington2001}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch47.signature-nfscan
\begin{physicalvignette}[Spherical near-field scanner with dual-polarization probes]
Walk through a spherical near-field facility before the transform runs: probe standoff, cable phase, RF leakage on the floor, and the absorption on the chamber walls. The transform can produce a beautiful modal table, yet the instrument rank is fixed by how many independent probe stations participate in the inversion \cite{hansen1988}. Six probes cannot resolve nine watt-distinguishable export directions even when the horn under test radiates them \cite{stratton1941}.
Reactive near-field scans on the same sphere often show vivid phase circulation while the outward power on a reference horn remains below the noise floor. That contrast is the everyday lesson of separating storage from export before attaching harmonic indices \cite{harrington2001}. Beth-balance and turntable checks remain the sanity tests that chart relabelling alone cannot replace \cite{beth1936}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch47.signature-nfscan} anchors the near-field scan signature used throughout the null-space and reconstruction discussion \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
Near-field campaigns therefore carry two parallel bound checks. The synthesis bound asks whether a declared feed on fixed \((\Omega_s,k_0)\) can populate a target pattern above tolerance on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$; the recovery bound asks whether a declared scan with $N$ probes can reconstruct that pattern from tangential samples on $\Gamma_R$ \cite{harrington2001}. Confusing the two is a common laboratory error in ``rich source, poor scan'': $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ may rank high while $\mathcal{M}_N$ remains probe-limited, leaving radiative channels in $\ker\mathcal{M}_N$ that no post-analysis can resurrect \cite{devaney2004}.
Laboratory memos must include $(N,\varepsilon,\mathcal{C}\text{ gauge})$ beside every recovery plot, and $(k_0 D_s,d_{\mathrm{eff}},\ell_{\mathrm{supp}})$ beside every synthesis plot \cite{poynting1884}. When the integers disagree, the honest report states \emph{which} operator bound failed---generation or observation---rather than attributing the gap to solver tolerance \cite{hansen1988}.
Physically, the rank-$N$ instrument readout $\mathcal{M}_N:\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}$ has rank at most $N$ regardless of feed complexity \cite{harrington2001}. The inaccessible subspace
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.signature-nfscan-null}
\ker\mathcal{M}_N\cap \operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega
\end{equation}
contains radiative channels nature radiates but the scanner cannot resolve \cite{devaney2004}. The laboratory implication is immediate: increasing feed complexity without increasing $N$ changes $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})$ but does not enlarge $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}(\mathcal{C})$ \cite{stratton1941}. Feed design may succeed while reconstruction fails because probe count, not Maxwell theory, sets the recovery ceiling on declared instruments \cite{devaney2004}.
\subsection{Non-radiating and unreachable modes}
\label{sec:ch471}
Non-radiating currents change near fields without altering far-zone patterns \cite{stratton1941}. Unreachable chart directions cannot be driven by any admissible feed on fixed support \cite{hansen1988}. Both phenomena occupy structural nullities of the radiation map \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega=\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{R}_\omega\) established in Section~\ref{sec:ch27} \cite{stratton1941}.

% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch471-b2

Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch471} \cite{stratton1941}.

Consider Open rectangular aperture in an infinite ground screen. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Non-radiating currents change near fields without altering far-zone patterns . Unreachable chart directions cannot be driven by any admissible feed on fixed support .
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.

The significance of this partition becomes clearer when four laboratory nouns are kept distinct in every compact-range measurement record. \emph{Invisible} modes arise from NR lifts in $\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega$: they alter near fields without changing far-zone $\mathbf{c}$ \cite{devaney2004}. \emph{Unreachable} modes lie in $\ker\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger}$ on the coefficient chart: they appear in a complete listing yet receive no watts from any admissible feed on fixed support \cite{stratton1941}. \emph{Unobservable} modes live in $\ker\mathcal{M}_N$ after finite sampling: nature radiates them, but the declared scan cannot resolve them \cite{harrington2001}. \emph{Padding} rows sit below $\varepsilon\sigma_{1}$ in the singular-value ordering: they are listed in $\mathcal{C}$ but carry no watt-bearing export \cite{jackson1999}. Membership tests---not MoM residuals or synthesis costs---decide which class obstructs a target \cite{hansen1988}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch471} quantifies how those nullities partition synthesis space. Near-field observables respond to $\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega$ and to unreachable coefficient directions, while $\mathbf{c}$ on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$ does not \cite{devaney2004}. Radiative rank counts reachable directions; nullity counts directions the chart lists but geometry cannot excite \cite{hansen1988}. Fix tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, flux-normalized $\mathcal{C}$ on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$, and compact $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ on $\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}$ \cite{stratton1941}. Non-radiating definitions use Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch2.nr-current-def} and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.synthesis-null-space} by reference \cite{harrington2001}.
Define the unreachable subspace
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.unreachable-modes}
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}=\ker\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger}\cap\mathbb{C}^{I},
\end{equation}
Physically, unreachable modes are coefficient components that no impressed current on the declared support can synthesize: they belong to the null space of export, not to imperfect optimization \cite{stratton1941}. Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.unreachable-modes} is the coefficient-side null space of the export adjoint---directions orthogonal to every image $\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]$, $\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}$ \cite{hansen1988}. On the range, this is the set of pattern components no passive source on $\Omega_{s}$ can radiate, regardless of feed optimization \cite{devaney2004}. Driving them on fixed geometry is physically impossible on fixed support, visible as persistent synthesis residual $\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}>0$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.synthesis-residual-norm}, regardless of MoM residual \cite{harrington2001}.
for ball support \(B_{a}\) at \(k_{0}a\gtrsim1\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.unreach-dim-bound}
\dim\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\ \ge\ (2\ell_{\max}+1)^{2}-d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)-\dim\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega,
\end{equation}
because listing cardinality exceeds radiative dimension whenever \(\ell_{\max}\) exceeds \(\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)\)
\cite{hansen1988}. On centered feeds, symmetry can force \(\dim\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\) to exceed half of
\(|\mathbb{C}^{I}|\) while \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\le4\) \cite{jackson1999}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.nr-lift-invisibility}
For \(\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{nr}}\in\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega\cap\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) and \(\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\),
\(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp+\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{nr}}]=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Near-field data distinguish \(\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{nr}}\); far-zone \(\mathbf{c}\) does not \cite{devaney2004}.
Linearity: \(\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp+\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{nr}}]=\mathcal{R}_\omega[\Jimp]\) since \(\mathcal{R}_\omega[\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{nr}}]=\mathbf{0}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Bounded \(\mathcal{C}\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) gives identical \(\mathbf{c}\) \cite{hansen1988}. Near-field
functionals on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ev}}\) retain NR sensitivity \cite{devaney2004}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.nr-lift-invisibility}) is the synthesis invisibility law: NR superposition is a current-space ambiguity, not a far-zone degree of
freedom \cite{stratton1941}. Inverse solvers that add NR templates alter near-field fits without changing \(\mathbf{c}\)
\cite{devaney2004}.
Synthesis non-uniqueness aggregates NR lifts and unreachable directions:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.nr-unreachable-composition}
\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{syn}}\supseteq\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega\oplus\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}},
\end{equation}
with bounds valid only on quotient \(\mathbb{C}^{I}/\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{syn}}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
Non-radiating sources lift invisible degrees of freedom in inversion: near-field data may fit reactive patterns that never enter $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$ \cite{devaney2004}. Far-zone rank reporting requires outgoing filtering before inversion \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{lem:ch4.nf-discrimination}
Distinct \({\Jimp}_{A},{\Jimp}_{B}\) with identical \(\mathbf{c}\) require near-field probes resolving \(\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega\) to discriminate
\cite{harrington2001}.
Reachable subspace projection. Every target decomposes as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.reachable-projection}
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{reach}}\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}+\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}},\qquad
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{reach}}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
Realizability requires \(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}=\mathbf{0}\) \cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.unreachable-obstruction}
If \(\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\|_{\mathcal{C}}>\varepsilon\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\|\), then
\(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\notin\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) at tolerance \(\varepsilon\), independent of feed optimization
\cite{stratton1941}.
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.reachable-projection}; unreachable component lies outside \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) for all \(\Jimp\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Synthesis residual Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.synthesis-residual-norm} lower-bounds \(\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\|\)
\cite{devaney2004}.
Unreachable weight is orthogonal to every admissible impressed-current portrait on $\Omega_{s}$ \cite{stratton1941}. For the source, no passive $\Jimp$ synthesizes $\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}$. In the far zone, export lies in $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ only. In measurement, MoM fit success does not erase $\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\|$ \cite{devaney2004}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.unreachable-obstruction}) closes the synthesis condition before any synthesis attempt: unreachable weight is orthogonal to every admissible
\(\Jimp\) portrait and therefore survives feed reweighting, adjoint iteration, and Tikhonov damping unchanged in the \(\mathcal{C}\)-gauge
\cite{stratton1941}. Tolerance reports must report \(\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\|/\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\|\)
Spherical near-field facilities and radio-telescope receivers encounter probe-limited recovery \cite{hansen1988}.
alongside MoM residuals \cite{hansen1988}.
on symmetric listings with \(\ell_{\max}\gg\ell_{\mathrm{supp}}(\varepsilon,k_{0}a)\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.unreach-dim-bound-symmetric}
\dim\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\ \ge\ |\mathbb{C}^{I}|-d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)-\dim\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega,
\end{equation}
so large nullity is expected rather than pathological when listing exceeds geometry-supported rank \cite{hansen1988}.
Compact \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) yields Fredholm alternative: \(\mathbf{c}\in\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) iff
\(\mathbf{c}\perp\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\) \cite{harrington2001}. Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.reachable-projection} is the finite-dimensional
realization \cite{devaney2004}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.quotient-synthesis}
\mathbf{c}_{1}=\mathbf{c}_{2}\ \text{in }\mathbb{C}^{I}/\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}
\iff
\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}(\mathbf{c}_{1}-\mathbf{c}_{2})=0.
\end{equation}
Uniqueness of \(\Jimp\) is not claimed; uniqueness of export class is \cite{harrington2001}.
Unreachable modes are chart directions no bounded impressed current can populate above tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. They differ from inaccessible modes, which synthesis might achieve but a finite scan cannot resolve \cite{stratton1941}.

changes near-field response \cite{devaney2004}.

Centered \(B_{a}\), \(k_{0}a=1\): \(\dim\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}/|\mathbb{C}^{I}|=0.55\), \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}=4\) \cite{hansen1988}.
Weak coupling is magnitude; unreachable nullity is membership \cite{hansen1988}. Sectors in \(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\)
have zero coupling to every finite \(\Jimp\), not merely small coupling \cite{devaney2004}. 
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.farzone-nr-blind}
Any measurement functional supported only on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) is blind to \(\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega\) on \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}.
\emph{Problem 4.7.1.} Centered electric dipole feed on open ball $B_{a}$; $k_{0}a=1$; chart $\ell\le12$; target sector $(5,3,\mathbf{M})$ with unit coefficient; tolerance $\varepsilon=10^{-2}$; aligned flux gauge; passive linear exterior. Use Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.unreachable-modes}, (\ref{thm:ch4.nr-lift-invisibility}), and (\ref{thm:ch4.unreachable-obstruction}).
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Decompose $\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}$ via Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.reachable-projection} and report $\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\|/\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\|$.
\item[(b)] Distinguish a non-radiating current lift from membership in $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}$ for the centered dipole feed.
\item[(c)] State whether $\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0$ is achievable and what feed or geometry change would be required.
\end{enumerate}
For part~(a), Set $\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}=\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{53}^{(\mathbf{M})}$ in the aligned gauge. Symmetry nulls on the centered dipole place the entire target in $\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{unreach}}$, so $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{reach}}\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}=\mathbf{0}$ and $\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{unreach}}\|/\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\|=1$. (\ref{thm:ch4.unreachable-obstruction}) rejects the target at any $\varepsilon<1$.
For part~(b), A non-radiating template $\mathbf{J}_{\mathrm{nr}}\in\ker\mathcal{R}_\omega$ alters near fields without changing $\mathbf{c}$ per (\ref{thm:ch4.nr-lift-invisibility}). Here the obstruction is export-side: odd azimuthal index $m=3$ on a centered feed is orthogonal to $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$, not weakly coupled. Near probes could fit reactive structure; far-zone $\mathbf{c}$ cannot acquire the $(5,3,\mathbf{M})$ weight.
For part~(c), $\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}>0$ for every admissible $\Jimp$ on centered $B_{a}$. Offset feeds, broken symmetry, or enlarged support that removes the coupling null are required; synthesis tolerance and MoM residual do not enter the membership test.
\subsection{Reactive subspaces and evanescent accessibility}
\label{sec:ch472}
Near-field scanners often report large reactive energy fractions near feeds; that energy is a storage budget on evanescent sheets, not radiative rank on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$ \cite{stratton1941}. Raising probe count may raise $\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}$ while $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ on fixed \((\Omega_s,k_0)\) stays unchanged---reactive $\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{acc}}^{(N)}$ records what the scan resolves locally, not how many propagating channels the outline can synthesize \cite{hansen1988}.
\medskip
Reactive probe rank is often misread as ``more near-field data implies more MIMO streams.' Evanescent sheets store energy and couple strongly to dense probe grids, raising $\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}$ without moving $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}$ on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$ after the propagating filter of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-filter} \cite{stratton1941}. The laboratory question is therefore bifurcated: how much reactive structure does the scan resolve locally, and how many propagating directions does the outline export at declared tolerance \cite{hansen1988}?
Standoff, grid pitch, and transverse wavenumber ceiling enter the reactive budget; electrical diameter and tail laws enter the export budget \cite{jackson1999}. Reports that conflate the two inflate stream counts and understate the geometry-side rank that synthesis must respect \cite{devaney2004}.
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch472
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch472-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Dual-reflector compact range feed]
A second bench observation matters on Synthetic aperture radar stripmap feed horn. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch472} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Dual-reflector compact range feed]
Consider Dual-reflector compact range feed. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Reactive storage is real near feeds but must not be reported as far-zone stream count . 
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch472} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.reactive-accessibility}
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}(\mathbf{c})=\big\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{react}}\mathbf{c}\big\|_{\mathrm{react}},
\end{equation}
with \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{react}}\) projecting reactive components when near data are used \cite{harrington2001}. Mathematically,
\(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) measures how much of the field chart lies outside \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) at probe resolution \(N\).
Let \(N_{\mathrm{ev}}(k_{t,\max})\) count evanescent modes with \(k_{t}>k_{0}\) resolved up to \(k_{t,\max}\) \cite{jackson1999}.
\(N_{\mathrm{ev}}\) grows with scan resolution while \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) remains fixed on
\((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\) \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{prop:ch4.reactive-not-synthesis}
\(\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) is independent of \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}. Raising \(N\) may raise \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) without enlarging
\(\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) \cite{devaney2004}.
Propagating-sector filter. Before listing \(\mathbf{c}\), apply
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.propagating-filter}
\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}=\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}\mathcal{C}\big[\mathcal{S}_\omega[\Jimp]\big],
\end{equation}
importing Section~\ref{sec:ch44} by reference \cite{stratton1941}. Scans that skip Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-filter} inflate
\(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) without changing \(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.ev-invisible-export}
Spherical near-field facilities and radio-telescope receivers encounter probe-limited recovery \cite{hansen1988}.
\(\|\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}\) and \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) are independent of
\(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) on fixed \((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\) \cite{stratton1941}.
(\ref{prop:ch4.reactive-not-synthesis}) and linearity of \(\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}\) \cite{stratton1941}. Evanescent
content lies outside \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
Evanescent near-field energy does not enlarge far-zone radiative rank \cite{stratton1941}. For the source, reactive storage on $\Omega_{s}$ is invisible to $\mathbf{c}$. In measurement, near scans resolve content filtered by $\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}$ before far-zone listing \cite{harrington2001}.
Evanescent richness at the scan plane does not increase propagating radiative rank \cite{harrington2001}. Seventy percent reactive energy in a near-field pattern is not seventy percent far-zone diversity \cite{hansen1988}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.reactive-radiative-split}
\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{react}}[\Jimp]=\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{react}}\mathcal{S}_\omega[\Jimp]\|,\quad
\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rad}}[\Jimp]=\|\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\mathcal{S}_\omega[\Jimp]\|_{\mathrm{rad}}.
\end{equation}
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.reactive-dominance}
\(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{react}}\gg\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) does not imply \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) enlargement on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\)
\cite{stratton1941}.
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-filter} removes evanescent weight before \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) \cite{stratton1941}. Reactive
dominance enlarges \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{react}}\) without altering \(\sigma_{n}\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{devaney2004}.
Large reactive near fields record storage, not radiative dimension \cite{stratton1941}. For the aperture, subwavelength hotspots do not buy extra far-zone modes. In measurement, tomography rank must not be reported as MIMO diversity \cite{devaney2004}.
Theorem~\ref{thm:ch4.reactive-dominance} states that tomography and hotspot scans record storage and probe resolution, not export
rank on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{stratton1941}. 
Transverse wavenumbers resolved at standoff \(z\) satisfy
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.ev-wavenumber-budget}
k_{t,\max}(z)\approx k_{0}+\frac{1}{z}\ln\!\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{probe}}}\right),
\end{equation}
so over-resolving \(k_{t}\) inflates \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) without shifting \(\mathbf{c}\) \cite{jackson1999}.
The laboratory configuration is a bounded source at fixed $\omega$ observed on $\Gamma_R$ with declared flux gauge. The relation below connects that situation to the coefficient description inherited from Chapter~3. \cite{stratton1941}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.react-export-ratio}
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{re}}=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}}{\|\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}+\delta},\qquad \delta>0.
\end{equation}
Large \(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{re}}\) with fixed \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) signals reactive-dominated recovery \cite{harrington2001}.
Near-field transfer matrix \(\mathbf{G}_{N}\) has rank \(\le N\); reactive recovery saturates when \(N\ge N_{\mathrm{ev}}(k_{t,\max})\)
\cite{harrington2001}. \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) is bounded by \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\), not \(\mathbf{G}_{N}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.reactive-energy-budget}
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}=\int_{\Sigma_{N}}\big(\varepsilon_{0}|\E|^{2}+\mu_{0}|\H|^{2}\big)\,dS,
\end{equation}
grows with \(N\) and \(k_{t,\max}\) but does not enter \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{jackson1999}.
Reactive dominance warns that input match and stored energy can grow while far-zone rank stays low \cite{stratton1941}. Compact antennas near resonance illustrate the split: high Q, low export dimension \cite{jackson1999}.
\phantomsection\label{lem:ch4.probe-sat-lemma}
\(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) saturates as \(N\) increases while \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) remains constant on fixed \((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\)
\cite{harrington2001}.

Probe-rank saturation. Physically, the display fixes a step that every compact-range campaign presupposes once outgoing export has been declared on the measurement sphere. \cite{stratton1941}

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}=\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{react}}\mathcal{S}_\omega[\Jimp]\|,\qquad
\mathbf{c}_{N\to\infty}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp].
\end{equation}
Saturation of \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) does not shift \(\mathbf{c}_{N\to\infty}\) \cite{stratton1941}.

Probe budgets should therefore be reported as \((N,\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)},d_{\mathrm{eff}})\) triples rather than as rank surrogates
\cite{hansen1988}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.prop-filter-persist}
\(\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}=\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\mathbf{c}\) is invariant under reactive probe enrichment on fixed \((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\)
\cite{stratton1941}.
For \(k_{t}>k_{0}\), probe coupling decays as \(\exp(-\alpha z)\) with standoff \(z\) \cite{jackson1999}. Over-resolving \(k_{t}\) without
reducing \(z\) inflates \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) without improving far-zone rank \cite{harrington2001}.
\(N=50\) on \(\lambda/4\) grid: \(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) within \(2\%\) of limit; \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(10^{-2})=7\) for \(N=25,50,100\)
\cite{harrington2001}.
 Enlarging one does not enlarge the other on passive supports
\cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.reactive-saturation}
\(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}\) saturates as \(N\) increases while \(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) remains constant on fixed \((\Omega_{s},k_{0})\)
\cite{harrington2001}.
\emph{Problem 4.7.2.} Near-field scan at standoff $z=\lambda/10$; transverse wavenumber resolved to $k_{t,\max}=2k_{0}$; measured reactive-to-radiative ratio $\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{re}}=2.4$; dipole-class source with $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(10^{-2})=5$ on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$; $N=50$ calibrated linear probes; passive exterior. Use Eqs.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-filter}, \eqref{eq:ch4.react-export-ratio}, and (\ref{thm:ch4.ev-invisible-export}).
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Explain why large $\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}$ does not raise $d_{\mathrm{eff}}$ on fixed $(\Omega_{s},k_{0})$.
\item[(b)] Apply Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.propagating-filter} and state whether $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}$ changes when $N$ increases from $25$ to $100$.
\item[(c)] Interpret $\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{re}}=2.4$ for a synthesis report that claims twelve independent far-zone streams.
\end{enumerate}
For part~(a), Evanescent content lies outside $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$. (\ref{thm:ch4.ev-invisible-export}) fixes $\|\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}\|_{\mathrm{rad}}$ and $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$ once $(\Omega_{s},k_{0})$ are declared; reactive probes record storage, not radiative rank.
For part~(b), After $\Pi_{\mathrm{rad}}\Pi_{\mathrm{prop}}$, $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}$ is invariant under probe enrichment on fixed geometry per Corollary~\ref{cor:ch4.prop-filter-persist}. Measured data show $\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{react}}^{(N)}$ within $2\%$ of its limit at $N=50$ while $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(10^{-2})=7$ for $N=25,50,100$ on the same source.
For part~(c), Twelve streams exceed $d_{\mathrm{eff}}=5$; the reactive ratio signals evanescent-dominated recovery, not MIMO diversity. Reactive energy in the scan plane must be filtered before stream count is reported on $\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}$.
\subsection{Synthesis, reconstruction, and inversion limits}
\label{sec:ch473}
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch473} states inversion limits as operator-range theorems for compact \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) \cite{hansen1988}. The
central distinction is variational fit versus range membership: variational fits find closest coefficients in \(\mathbb{C}^{I}\); synthesis demands zero
perpendicular residual \(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.inversion-residual-decomposition} \cite{stratton1941}. A MoM
residual below \(10^{-4}\) is therefore necessary but not sufficient for \(\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0\) \cite{devaney2004}.
pattern synthesis minimizes a cost on $\mathbb{C}^{I}$; realizability demands membership in $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$. A MoM residual of $10^{-4}$ can coexist with $\|\mathbf{r}_\perp\|/\|\mathbf{c}\|=0.15$ when the target carries unreachable weight---the variational fit finds the closest pattern, not a synthesizable one \cite{stratton1941}. The bound check terminates on Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.range-certificate}, not on adjoint scores or Tikhonov norms \cite{hansen1988}.
\medskip
Inverse-design excitation-to-flux sequences excel at minimizing costs on $\mathbb{C}^{I}$, yet $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}$ is a range-membership statement on $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$ \cite{stratton1941}. Adjoint gradients, Tikhonov paths, and MoM residuals are indispensable diagnostics---they trace \emph{how close} a target lies to the reachable set---but they cannot establish $\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0$ when $\mathbf{r}_\perp\neq0$ \cite{hansen1988}.
Synthesis claims close on Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.range-certificate}: publish $\|\mathbf{r}_\perp\|/\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\|$ alongside any synthesis cost, and treat persistent perpendicular weight as a geometry obstruction to be resolved by support enlargement or target revision, not by deeper iteration \cite{devaney2004}.
Use (\ref{thm:ch4.moore-penrose-synthesis}) and (\ref{prin:ch4.operator-composition}) \cite{harrington2001}.
Discrete MoM matrix \(\mathbf{T}\) is a Galerkin restriction of \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) on \(\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}\) \cite{harrington2001}.
Tolerance \(\varepsilon\in(0,1)\) fixed; flux-normalized \(\mathcal{C}\) on \(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{rad}}\) \cite{hansen1988}. Unreachable
nullity from Subsection~\ref{sec:ch471} is imported by reference only \cite{devaney2004}.
Every target \(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\in\mathbb{C}^{I}\) admits the orthogonal split
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch473
% PHYSICAL-MEMORY:sec:ch473-b2
\begin{physicalvignette}[Metrology-grade open-ended waveguide probe]
A second bench observation matters on Compact enclosure with multiple coupled patches. When technicians swap only the receive probe while holding the transmit chain fixed, reciprocity checks whether the reported coupling is property of the structure or artifact of a mismatched flux gauge \cite{stratton1941}. Failed reciprocity usually traces to a near-field tap, a radome standing-wave, or a probe polarization basis that was never aligned with the transmit standard \cite{hansen1988}.
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
The measurement logic above is made precise in Subsection~\ref{sec:ch473} \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{physicalvignette}[Metrology-grade open-ended waveguide probe]
Consider Metrology-grade open-ended waveguide probe. Before any coefficient table is printed, technicians record frequency, cable loss, and the mechanical outline that fixes electrical size. The first walk-through of the range is always tactile: flange bolts, probe standoff, turntable boresight, and the wattmeter reference on the receive chain \cite{hansen1988}.
On this hardware the near zone is visually impressive---standing reactive structure, rapid phase rotation, sometimes a visible VSWR ripple at the feed---yet the export question is measured only after outgoing selection on a large sphere \cite{harrington2001}. What the camera sees in the chamber is not automatically what the far-zone pattern claims \cite{stratton1941}.
Engineers therefore learn to separate three readings: what the feed can synthesize on declared support, what propagating content survives on the radiative range, and what a finite instrument can recover at declared tolerance \cite{devaney2004}. Confusing those readings produces the rank gaps and watt failures that later sections quantify \cite{pal2026}.
Small method-of-moments residuals can mask large unreachable components . Synthesis success requires zero null projection, not small least squares .
Chu and Landau warned that finite size caps simultaneous localization and spectral spread \cite{chu1948}. Devaney's inverse-scattering perspective reminds us that reachable far-zone data never exceeds what bounded sources can export \cite{devaney2004}.
Subsection~\ref{sec:ch473} states the formal export condition that this range scene presupposes \cite{stratton1941}.
\end{physicalvignette}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.inversion-residual-decomposition}
\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}+\mathbf{r}_{\perp},\qquad
\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\in\ker\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger},
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger}\) is the Moore--Penrose range projector on the
coefficient chart \cite{harrington2001}. Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.inversion-residual-decomposition} is the Fredholm split for
compact \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\). \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\) is the closest realizable pattern;
\(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\) is the unreachable component no passive \(\Jimp\) can radiate \cite{devaney2004}.
synthesizable is equivalent to membership in \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\):
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.range-certificate}
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})
\iff
\exists\,\Jimp\in\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{real}}:\ \boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega[\Jimp]=\mathbf{c}
\iff
\mathbf{r}_{\perp}=\mathbf{0}.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:ch4.range-certificate} is the synthesis condition every inversion report must close \cite{stratton1941}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.inversion-limit}
Best rank-\(d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)\) approximation to \(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\) achieves error
\(\le\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\|\) on \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\). If
\(\|\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{C}}>\varepsilon\|\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\|\), then
\(\mathbf{c}_{\mathrm{target}}\notin\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\) at tolerance \(\varepsilon\), independent of synthesis trial or feed count
\cite{stratton1941}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.radiative-rank}) bounds truncation error on \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) by \(\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\)
\cite{harrington2001}. Component \(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\in\ker\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger}\) lies outside range for all ranks
\cite{devaney2004}. Combining Eckart--Young optimality with Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.inversion-residual-decomposition} yields the obstruction clause
\cite{hansen1988}.
(\ref{thm:ch4.inversion-limit}) elevates inversion from a numerical success criterion to a geometric membership test \cite{stratton1941}. No
amount of feed reweighting, adjoint iteration, or Tikhonov damping moves \(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\) into \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\)
when \(\delta_{\mathrm{reg}}>0\) in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.tikhonov-synthesis-gap} \cite{harrington2001}.
Inversion limits are what a finite probe count and noise floor allow one to recover---not what Maxwell lists in a complete chart \cite{devaney2004}. Regularization residuals must be reported beside inferred $\widehat{c}_{\ell m}^{(\sigma)}$ \cite{stratton1941}.
Tikhonov--synthesis gap. Regularized inversion minimizes
\(\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{I}_{s}-\mathbf{c}\|^{2}+\alpha\|\mathbf{I}_{s}\|^{2}\) but range membership is established only by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.tikhonov-synthesis-gap}
\delta_{\mathrm{reg}}=\|\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\|_{\mathcal{C}},\qquad
\delta_{\mathrm{reg}}=0\iff\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C}).
\end{equation}
\phantomsection\label{cor:ch4.reg-not-realizable}
\(\alpha>0\) lowers discrete residuals without moving \(\mathbf{c}\) into \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) when \(\delta_{\mathrm{reg}}>0\)
\cite{harrington2001}.
\phantomsection\label{thm:ch4.discrete-continuum-gap}
\(\|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{I}_{s}-\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathcal{C}}<\tau_{\mathrm{mom}}\) does not imply \(\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0\) unless
\(\tau_{\mathrm{mom}}<\varepsilon\|\mathbf{c}\|\) and \(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}=\mathbf{0}\) in the continuum gauge of \(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\)
\cite{harrington2001}.
Galerkin discretization error is bounded by mesh resolution on \(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\) \cite{harrington2001}. Unreachable
\(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\) is gauge-independent in \(\ker\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
on truncated rank \(d\),
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.mom-penrose-chain}
\mathbf{I}_{s}^{(d)}=\mathbf{T}_{d}^{\dagger}\mathbf{c}
=\sum_{n=1}^{d}\frac{\langle\mathbf{u}_{n},\mathbf{c}\rangle}{\sigma_{n}}\,\mathbf{v}_{n},
\end{equation}
where \(\mathbf{T}_{d}=\sum_{n=1}^{d}\sigma_{n}\mathbf{u}_{n}\mathbf{v}_{n}^{\dagger}\) \cite{harrington2001}. Equation
\eqref{eq:ch4.mom-penrose-chain} inverts only the reachable component \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}^{(d)}\mathbf{c}\); it cannot synthesize
\(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\) \cite{stratton1941}.
for rank-\(d\) truncation, \(\kappa(\mathbf{T}_{d})=\sigma_{1}/\sigma_{d}\). Large \(\kappa\) amplifies noise in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.mom-penrose-chain}
without reducing \(\|\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\|\) \cite{hansen1988}. Synthesis checks must report \((\delta_{\mathrm{syn}},\kappa,d_{\mathrm{eff}})\)
as a triple membership test \cite{stratton1941}.
Rank-truncated inversion bound. Best rank-\(d_{\mathrm{eff}}\) inverse satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.rank-truncated-inverse}
\min_{\mathbf{I}_{s}}\|\mathbf{T}_{d}\mathbf{I}_{s}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}\mathbf{c}\|
\le
\varepsilon\sigma_{1}\|\mathbf{c}\|,
\end{equation}
but cannot reduce \(\|\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\|\) \cite{harrington2001}. Truncation optimizes within $\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega$; it does not enlarge that range on fixed geometry
\cite{devaney2004}.
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ch4.eckart-young-target}
\|\mathbf{c}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}^{(d_{\mathrm{eff}})}\mathbf{c}\|_{\mathcal{C}}\le\varepsilon\|\mathbf{c}\|
\iff
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}^{(d_{\mathrm{eff}})}\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{real}}(\mathcal{C})\ \text{at }\varepsilon.
\end{equation}
Equality in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.eckart-young-target} still fails when \(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\neq0\) \cite{harrington2001}.
Adjoint synthesis schemes minimize \(\|\mathbf{T}^{H}\mathbf{c}-\mathbf{w}\|\) on feed weights \(\mathbf{w}\) \cite{harrington2001}. The adjoint
finds a closest current portrait whose export \(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{I}_{s}\) may fit \(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ran}}\mathbf{c}\) while leaving
\(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\) in the target unchanged \cite{stratton1941}. The tolerance test requires lifting to Eq.~\eqref{eq:ch4.range-certificate},
not reporting adjoint residual alone \cite{harrington2001}.
Discrete probe grids approximate continuum angular spectra; the gap is discretization error, not new physics \cite{stratton1941}. Refining the grid improves recovery until $N\ge d_{\mathrm{eff}}(\varepsilon)$, then saturates \cite{devaney2004}.
\phantomsection\label{lem:ch4.sparsity-obstruction}
\(\ell_{1}\) or cardinality priors on \(\mathbf{I}_{s}\) alter the variational setting of inversion but do not enlarge
\(\operatorname{ran}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega\). If \(\mathbf{r}_{\perp}\neq0\), no sparsity penalty yields \(\delta_{\mathrm{syn}}=0\)
\cite{harrington2001}.

The laboratory implication is that optimization can drive the MoM residual toward zero while the unreachable component $\mathbf{r}_{\perp}$ remains fixed: synthesis tolerance requires the perpendicular part to vanish in $\ker\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_\omega^{\dagger}$, not merely a small matrix residual \cite{stratton1941}.
